93% of Audited Merchant Accounts Missed Visa CEDP Discounts, Verisave and Canyon Payments Analysis Finds

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Report finds most audited accounts are still paying penalty rates and traces the losses to data-flow errors merchants can fix without switching processors

SALT LAKE CITY, Nov. 19, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- A year after Visa began enforcing its Commercial Enhanced Data Program (CEDP), many businesses that accept commercial cards are still paying higher interchange rates on transactions that could qualify for discounts. A new report from Verisave, a payments optimization and cost-reduction firm, produced with Canyon Payments, a payment services and integration partner, finds that 93% of 180 merchant accounts Verisave analyzed in a single month missed CEDP discounts. Together, they lost more than $800,000 in savings that month.

The report, "State of the Industry: Visa CEDP," finds that the payments industry moved quickly to adapt when Visa introduced CEDP in 2025, but the businesses paying the fees largely did not. Visa began charging a 5-basis-point participation fee on Level 2 and Level 3 volume in April 2025 and switched on automated data validation in October 2025. Level 2 rates for small business cards ended in January 2026, and Level 2 ended for corporate and purchasing cards in April 2026.

The penalty for falling short is steep. A $500 commercial or purchasing card transaction that qualifies for Visa's verified Product 3 rate costs about $8.85 in interchange (including the 5-basis-point participation fee). Downgraded to the non-compliant rate, the same transaction still incurs the participation fee and costs $13.60, an increase of 54%. Those downgrades land on top of the record $198.25 billion U.S. merchants paid in card processing fees in 2025, according to Nilson Report data cited by the Merchants Payments Coalition.

"A year in, the businesses most affected by CEDP are the ones that have done the least about it," said Jeremy Layton, Founder of Verisave. "Many finance teams saw their costs go up and assumed it was simply the new price of accepting commercial cards. Usually it isn't. The data is breaking somewhere between the merchant's systems and the processor, and that can be fixed without switching providers."

Some businesses have responded by passing the cost on to customers. One Verisave client that was losing nearly $500,000 a month to CEDP cited the program as the main reason it began surcharging card payments. The report notes a limit to that approach: card brand rules cap surcharges at 3%, and a business that misses CEDP rates is likely to end up with an effective rate above that cap. Surcharging alone can't recover the full cost.

The report finds that most failures are structural rather than the result of merchant error or fraud.

"In the accounts Verisave reviewed, 93% missed discounts they had already earned," said Canyon Payments CEO Alex Burgin. "We look at the payload, not the rate. When we open up the transactions, the failure is almost always structural: order totals that don't add up to the line items, commodity codes sent as plain text, Level 3 detail captured up front and dropped before settlement. That is not a pricing problem. It is a data problem, and once you can see exactly where it breaks, the fix is usually straightforward."

The full "State of the Industry: Visa CEDP" report is available at https://verisave.com/2026-visa-cedp-industry-report

About VerisaveVerisave is a payments optimization and cost-reduction firm that helps mid-market and enterprise businesses lower credit card processing fees without switching processors. Led by CEO Chris Lefebvre and founded by Jeremy Layton, Chief Commercial Officer, Verisave identifies setup errors, data-flow problems and misclassified transaction types that inflate processing costs, typically reducing fees by 10% to 30%. The firm has identified more than $3 billion in savings and works with clients including B&H Photo and GoDaddy. Learn more at verisave.com.

About Canyon PaymentsCanyon Payments is a payment services and integration partner for e-commerce and card-not-present businesses. Led by CEO Alex Burgin and CRO Jeff Hunsaker, who spent a combined 27 years at Visa, Canyon Payments helps merchants get more from the payment stack they already have, with zero-downtime switching and the integrations and custom development that fix the data problems behind downgrades. Canyon is a Certified Visa Acceptance Partner. Learn more at canyonpayments.com.

In HelloNation, Business Performance Expert Martin Rowan of Naperville, IL, Explains How SAP Execution Impacts Operational Stability

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

NAPERVILLE, Ill., Nov. 19, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- What happens when SAP stops being used merely to explain results and starts being used to drive them? That question is addressed in a HelloNation article featuring insights from Martin Rowan, Managing Partner at Reveal USA, Inc. in Naperville, IL. The article explores how organizations can strengthen operational stability by moving beyond using SAP as a simple reporting layer and fully leveraging it for both planning and execution.

The article notes that many businesses still treat SAP as a reporting layer, pulling data into spreadsheets to make decisions. This practice, while common, often leads to fragmented workflows and reduced accountability. As highlighted in the HelloNation piece, when planning and execution occur outside SAP, teams face longer decision-making delays and greater inconsistencies across departments. This increase in decision latency can negatively affect overall business performance, leaving organizations to react to problems rather than prevent them.

According to the article, SAP execution becomes far more effective when it is positioned at the center of both planning and operational processes. Rowan explains that teams relying on spreadsheets miss out on version control, automated checks, and system-wide integration. These gaps lead to avoidable errors, duplicate work, and misaligned strategies. The article underscores that restoring execution in SAP enhances control, speeds decision-making, and enables proactive management of business operations.

The HelloNation article emphasizes that a fully integrated SAP environment improves both decision speed and outcome reliability. When SAP execution is prioritized, teams can access real-time information that empowers them to act faster and with more confidence. This operational visibility also increases accountability, as everyone works from the same data source. Business performance becomes easier to manage because processes are consistent, and service levels stabilize across the board.

Another important takeaway from the article is the financial benefit of aligning planning and execution within SAP. Companies can free up working capital by streamlining procurement and inventory strategies. When execution decisions are based on current SAP data, rather than disconnected spreadsheet estimates, unnecessary expenditures are reduced. SAP becomes not only a planning tool but a driver of financial efficiency and operational effectiveness.

As the article explains, the shift from using SAP as a reporting layer to a full planning and execution system requires more than just technology. It demands a cultural shift within organizations. Teams must be willing to trust SAP to manage operational tasks in real time. With this shift, companies reduce decision latency, improve transparency, and make faster, more informed decisions. This kind of commitment leads to measurable gains in business performance and long-term stability.

The HelloNation feature also highlights that employees benefit from this transformation. By eliminating the need to reconcile conflicting spreadsheets, teams can focus on tasks that add value to the business. With SAP execution in place, operations become more predictable and efficient, improving the experience for both employees and customers alike.

The article titled "When SAP Stops Explaining Results and Starts Delivering Them" features insights from Martin Rowan, Business Performance Expert in Naperville, IL, in HelloNation.

Through its community-focused publications and innovative "edvertising" approach, HelloNation delivers content that informs, inspires, and spotlights the leaders making a meaningful impact in their communities.

Carnevali, serve rispondere a Conte per dare forza a Spalletti

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

«Se Spalletti vince l’Europa League deve rimanere alla Juventus e Carnevali non aspetta altro». Stefano Impallomeni, nella discussione sulla panchina della Juventus, offre una riflessione che non passa inosservata.

Dopo la candidatura di Conte, il dibattito sul futuro tecnico della Juve è diventato centrale. Il valore dell’ex allenatore del Napoli è fuori discussione: quello che ha fatto e vinto ovunque sia andato è sotto gli occhi di tutti. Così come è nota la sua metodologia di lavoro e la sua non propensione a essere un allenatore aziendalista, un atteggiamento che spesso ha portato a rotture con i club. È vero che il suo modo di operare ha prodotto risultati, ma è altrettanto vero che il contesto calcistico italiano oggi non ha più la potenza economica di qualche anno fa. Per questo, se vuoi guidare un club come la Juventus, che deve rispettare determinati paletti, devi essere disposto a scendere a compromessi.

Nel discorso sulla panchina bianconera è entrato anche Inzaghi. Quest’ultimo, infatti, a differenza di quanto sostiene Conte, è subentrato proprio ad Antonio all’Inter e, nonostante qualche sacrificio sul piano tecnico, ha vinto. Soprattutto, è arrivato per due volte in finale di Champions League, costruendo risultati importanti anche attraverso l'utilizzo di giocatori arrivati a parametro zero. Un tecnico che lavora in maniera diversa, ma che sa comunque produrre risultati. Anche la qualità di Inzaghi è fuori discussione e, come ho già avuto modo di dire, lo vedrei molto bene alla Juventus, soprattutto con la gestione Carnevali.

Ma, tornando all'osservazione iniziale di Impallomeni, non possiamo trascurare un aspetto: in caso di vittoria dell'Europa League da parte di Spalletti, cambiare guida tecnica diventerebbe molto più complicato. La Juve tornerebbe a vincere un trofeo internazionale che manca dal 1996 e Carnevali, alla sua prima stagione in bianconero, si ritroverebbe subito una coppa in bacheca potendo programmare il futuro in maniera più forte economicamente.

La Juventus è ancora in ballo in questa stagione e, proprio per questo, il lavoro del tecnico va rispettato. Carnevali, dopo le parole di Conte sul chiudere il cerchio alla Juve, deve far sentire la propria voce e dare forza a Spalletti e alla squadra. Non si può rischiare un altro bagno di sangue senza Champions. Il futuro è oggi, il domani può attendere.

Trump’s accounts traded up to $97.5M in Nvidia stock, CNBC finds

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One sale of Trump’s Nvidia stock came five days after Marco Rubio reportedly briefed him on Jensen Huang’s ties to a suspected Chinese spy. The White House says independent advisers run the portfolio.

Jensen Huang, then Nvidia’s CEO, speaks as President Donald Trump looks on at an ‘Investing in America’ event in the Cross Hall of the White House, 30 April 2025.

President Donald Trump’s accounts have bought or sold Nvidia shares at least 103 times since January 2025, a CNBC analysis found. Luke Fountain’s review of the financial disclosures, updated through August, values the trades at $27M to $97.5M in total.

The filings give dollar ranges rather than share counts. By the end of 2025, Nvidia was one of Trump’s three largest individual stock holdings, worth $12.5M to $58M. A year earlier, the stake was worth $615,000 to $1.3M.

One sale, of $500,001 to $1M, came on 18 May. That was the first Monday after Trump returned from his state visit to China, which Jensen Huang joined. Five days earlier, Secretary of State Marco Rubio had briefed Trump on intelligence about Huang’s relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, The New York Times reported.

The White House disputed the Times report without elaborating, the paper said. Trump denied being briefed when asked about it on 16 November.

“I haven’t heard that,” Trump said, according to CNBC.

He added that he did not know whether the woman, Alice Huang, was a Chinese spy. His accounts also bought smaller amounts of Nvidia stock in the days before and after the May sale. Trump disclosed more than 21,000 trades in 2025, CNBC found, against about 500 in his whole first term.

CNBC set Trump’s Nvidia trades against Huang’s growing access. The two have met or appeared together at least 15 times since Trump returned to office, by CNBC’s count. They first met at the White House on 31 January 2025. Two days earlier, Trump’s money managers bought $1M to $5M of Nvidia stock.

In August 2025, Trump confirmed a deal giving the US government 15% of Nvidia’s revenue from chip sales to China. A week later, his accounts bought $5M to $25M of Nvidia shares.

On 8 December, Trump said Nvidia could sell its more powerful H200 chips in China. His accounts had sold up to $1M of Nvidia stock four days earlier, and bought more the day after.

From January to August 2026, the accounts made 38 more Nvidia trades, worth $9.4M to $28.3M. On 8 October, Trump gave Huang the National Medal of Science. A disclosure released that day showed August purchases of up to $1.6M and sales of up to $1M.

A White House spokesperson told CNBC that independent advisers manage Trump’s portfolio without his input.

The arrangement aims to “avoid even the appearance of a conflict of interest,” Trump Organization spokesperson Kimberly Benza told CNBC.

CNBC noted that the disclosures offer no evidence that Trump directed the trades. Nor do they show that his advisers had access to classified intelligence. TNW has not independently verified the analysis.

Huang resigned as Nvidia’s CEO on 12 November. Trump has since backed his return, which Nvidia’s board is reportedly not considering.

NFL predictions: Our picks for Giants-Jaguars, the full Week 11 slate

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The New York Giants face major test this week when they face the Jacksonville Jaguars. It is also a game that will provide a huge swing in the Giants' expected chances to make the playoffs. Will the Giants upset Jacksonville? See what our Big Blue View staff members think about that game and the rest of the Week 11 action in this week's staff picks.

I think there's a path to a win for the Giants, if they can find it. The Giants are one of the very best teams in the NFL at forcing drives to end in punts. At the same time, the Jaguars will be without their starting center and the Giants are one of the best teams in the League at creating quick pressure. That's important because Trevor Lawrence has been one of the quarterbacks most affected by pressure in the NFL.

At the same time, the Giants have rediscovered the explosive pass with J.J. McCarthy, which has been the weakness of the Jags' defense.

If the Giants can score touchdowns while disrupting Lawrence, they've got the chance to come away with the upset. We've seen the Giants rebound after bad games, as well as play much better than anticipated when they're underdogs. Maybe both will happen here.

The flip side of the coin is simpler and scarier to me: The Giants have one of the worst red zone defenses in the NFL, and the Jaguars have one of the best red zone offenses in the league. If the Giants can't stop them between the 20s, this game could get ugly fast.

Jacksonville is one of the hottest teams in the league with a young and explosive offense that can run the football. Their defense is aggressive but doesn't NEED to bring the blitz — they blitz at about a league-average rate, but Anthony Campanile does an excellent job utilizing simulated pressure to harass opposing quarterbacks. Jacksonville is third in offensive EPA, 4th in defensive EPA allowed, and 1st in Net EPA — they're a juggernaut based on their performance and the advanced analytics.

Still, that doesn't mean the Giants don't have a chance — they just must play efficient football and avoid the catastrophic mistakes that have robbed them of wins. It's possible, but against the most efficient team in the NFL, it will be difficult. John Harbaugh will have the Giants ready and prepared for the game, and I believe it will be more exciting and competitive. Jacksonville is concluding their third straight road game, which could give the Giants a slight advantage at home, but I ultimately believe the better team finds a way to win.

It took 11 weeks, but for the first time this season, the Giants will face a team with a winning record when the 7-2 Jaguars come to town on Sunday. Not only is Jacksonville running away with the AFC South (they lead the division by four games), but they're steamrolling the competition. They've won their last three games by a combined 75 points. This is easily Big Blue's toughest test of the season, so far.

Jacksonville is very good on both sides of the ball. They're fourth in offensive DVOA, third in defensive DVOA, and third in overall DVOA. They also have an excellent field goal kicker with crazy range. If you look at both rosters, it's hard to find clear advantages for the Giants at any position group outside of tight end, and even there, Jacksonville has good players.

Can the Giants win this game? Yes. They're at home, they're getting an extra three days of rest, and as we saw when they played Houston, they aren't scared and can surprise as a big underdog. They'll need to dial it up on defense and pressure Trevor Lawrence into the kinds of mistakes he used to make before Liam Coen got there. They also might have a little extra motivation after letting last week's game against the Commanders slip away at the end.

I won't be surprised if John Harbaugh's team hangs in there and keeps it as a one-score game most of the way. But in the end, until the Giants show me that they can beat a really good team, I can't pick them to do so.

You can talk yourself into believing that the Giants can beat Jacksonville this Sunday. They needed a miraculous last-second 57-yard field goal to beat the Eagles team (with Jalen Hurts) that the Giants defeated (without Hurts). They lost to the Texans team that the Giants beat the following week. The woeful Titans were within a TD against them last week with 5 minutes left. So indeed, it could be a competitive game. Unfortunately, the Jags have also won games by 24, 29, 35, and 23 points this season. They have scored 31 or more in each of their past four games, and over 40 in two of those. The Giants have only scored more than 30 points once, and only then because of a 99-yard pick-6 on the last play of the game.

Trevor Lawrence isn't necessarily playing any better this year than in the past. He's a QB that simultaneously makes great passes and puts the ball up for grabs, yet unlike in years past, he's cut down on the INTs while getting the TDs. Part of that may be due to 5'10" wide receiver Parker Washington (a 2023 6th-round pick), who had a breakout 2025 and is on pace for 1,000 yards. Running back Bhayshul Tuten, a 4th round 2025 pick, isn't quite in the Jahmyr Gibbs – Kenneth Walker class but he's not far below and is a missed tackle machine. A lot of the credit has to go to head coach Liam Coen, who also calls the plays and turned this team around as soon as he was hired. Lawrence, like most QBs, has trouble with pressure, but he is not too fazed by the blitz. Finding a way to disrupt his rhythm is a key to any chance the Giants may have.

On defense, the Jaguars are strong at linebacker with fourth-round 2023 pick Ventrell Miller, one of the league's best (note how many mid-round picks the Jags have hit on), along with Foyesade Oluokun. They also put on a decent pass rush with Josh Hines-Allen, Travon Walker, and Dennis Gardeck. In the secondary, Travis Hunter is playing more like a No. 2 pick, having mostly given up his evening gig as a receiver.

Are the Giants a serious playoff contender? This is the type of game they can use to make a statement against a team that is in the Super Bowl conversation. I think they'll put up a fight. I also don't think they're there yet.

I want to convince myself to pick the Giants to defeat the Jaguars. I really do. I just can't. The Jaguars are a team with justifiable Super Bowl ambitions. They are a well-rounded team with a high-powered offense, a very good defense, and one of the best field-goal kickers in the business in Cam Little.

The Giants are a good team. They are resilient. They will give the Jaguars their best shot. Maybe, just maybe, it will be good enough to pull off an upset that would go a long way toward ensuring that the Giants make the playoffs.

Jackson 5 Member Marlon Jackson Reveals Stage 4 Prostate Cancer Diagnosis

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Jackson 5 member Marlon Jackson has revealed that he's been battling prostate cancer for the last four years ... and currently it's stage 4.

He revealed his diagnosis on Farah Shammas' podcast, saying ... "I've had cancer for four years."

Despite everything, Marlon said he's doing well, adding ... "I'm great. I'm good. Bad grass don't die. So, I'm going to be around for a long time."

Marlon also shared ... "I've had my prostate gland removed and I've had radiation treatment and everything is fine. Everything is great. I walk almost four miles four times a week. You know, and that's great for me, and eating healthy food."

After cutting out sugar from his diet, Marlon says his doctor told him ... "You're doing so much better than what I thought."

He added ... "[I'm] careful what I put in my body and try to take care of myself."

Keychron’s keyboards and dock are perfect desk accessories, and they’re up to $48 off

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

A good keyboard is essential to a good desk setup, and a Thunderbolt 5 dock makes that setup feel much cleaner. Keychron’s keyboards and new dock are on sale with up to $48 off during the deal season.

I’ve been using a Keychron Q3 HE for quite some time now. Over the last year or two, I’ve made a lateral move to Gateron Dawn double-rail magnetic switches from the stock Nebula switches it came with. Now, Keychron sells a Q3 HE with improved hardware for an 8K polling rate and with Lime silent switches by default.

It’s an excellent keyboard, and my only complaint is that the white variant I have doesn’t exactly match my desk.

Most of Keychron’s deals are good right now, but the standout is the Thunderbolt 5 Dock, which was released earlier this year. I have mine plugged into my Mac Mini for the time being, but I use it on my Windows PC and Googlebook without issue. The Thunderbolt 5 rating means it keeps up with next-gen speeds, but it’s backward-compatible and works with most devices. Just plug in a host device, and you have access to data and displays easily.

The Keychron Thunderbolt 5 Dock is down to $314, instead of $349. Considering it just launched earlier in the year, now is a good time to get one if you’re looking for a docking option.

Right now, Keychron has a number of deals running that are worth checking out.

The 8K Q3 HE magnetic keyboard from Keychron is down to $206 at 10% off. It was already a great deal for a fully aluminum case with good keycaps and excellent switches, but the new Black Friday deal sweetens the pot a little.

The classic Q3 HE is no slouch, though. I’d recommend it in an instant, and it’s down to $191 instead of $239. That’s $48 down from the traditional price, and a much better deal than the 8K version if you don’t absolutely need the insanely high polling rate.

The K4 HE is another great option, and it’s a much friendlier $81 on Amazon. That’s down from the $115 it normally is. It’s another hall-effect (magnetic switch) keyboard, but with wood accents and in a smaller 60% layout.

Keychron’s deals won’t last into next month, ending on November 30.

Ubisoft cuts jobs at major Assassin's Creed and Far Cry studios as its year of layoffs continues

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ubisoft has confirmed more layoffs as part of its previously announced operational "reset", this time affecting 166 employees at its major Montréal and Québec City studios - the teams responsible for game series including Assassin's Creed and Far Cry. It says the cuts are necessary "to ensure our resources are aligned with current production priorities and business needs."

GamesIndustry.biz reports the cuts - believed to equate to around four percent of the studios' total headcount - will impact employees working on projects either recently cancelled or significantly changed.

The publication also understands the cuts, said to primarily impact production and support roles at Ubisoft Montréal and Québec City, include members of Vantage Studios - Ubisoft and Tencent's joint development venture announced last October, which focuses on the publisher's biggest franchises, including Far Cry, Rainbow Six Siege, and Assassin's Creed.

In a statement provided to Eurogamer, Ubisoft confirmed it is, "reducing its workforce by 166 positions across its Montréal and Québec City operations", adding: "As our portfolio evolves, our production needs are changing, requiring us to adjust the size of some teams to ensure our resources are aligned with current production priorities and business needs.

"We deeply value the contributions of these teams and recognise the impact this may have on our colleagues," the statement continued. "We are committed to supporting them through this process with care and respect. Those affected are being provided with severance, extended health care benefits, career transition services and other resources."

These latest job cuts are part of a "major organisational, operational and portfolio reset" announced by Ubisoft in January. That initial announcement was accompanied by the news of two studio closures - affecting its Halifax mobile and Stockholm teams - plus the cancellation of six projects, including the much-delayed Prince of Persia: The Sands of Time remake.

Since then, 105 jobs have been cut at Red Storm Entertainment, the Ubisoft studio behind the Ghost Recon series, and around 380 employees were reportedly laid off when the publisher's Winnipeg and Belgrade teams shut in June. In October, Ubisoft closed Trials developer RedLynx and downsized its Singapore Assassin's Creed: Black Flag Resynced team, impacting a total of approximately 120 employees. Amid all this, Ubisoft developers have been on strike in response to the publisher's sweeping layoffs.

Mac, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

PC, PS4, PS5, Stadia, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Valvoline (VVV) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Management outlines fiscal 2027 targets and a new national advertising push.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:43PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Valvoline (VVV +12.60%) management reported results for the fourth quarter and fiscal year 2026, highlighted by the 20th consecutive year of system-wide same-store sales growth. The company reported the expansion of its retail network to 2,517 total stores and surpassed $4 billion in annual system-wide sales for the first time. Financial performance was influenced by a 60% increase in finished lubricant costs, which management addressed through pricing actions and operational efficiencies. For fiscal 2027, the company provided guidance anticipating continued revenue and EBITDA growth alongside the launch of a national advertising fund to drive customer acquisition.

Operator: Hello, everyone. Thank you for joining us, and welcome to Valvoline's Third (sic) [Fourth] Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] I will now hand the conference over to Elizabeth Clevinger, Investor Relations. Elizabeth, please go ahead.

Elizabeth Clevinger: Thank you. Good morning, and welcome to Valvoline's Fourth Quarter Fiscal 2026 Conference Call and Webcast. This morning, Valvoline released results for the fourth quarter and fiscal year ended September 30, 2026. This presentation should be viewed in conjunction with that earnings release, a copy of which is available on our Investor Relations website at investors.valvoline.com. Please note that these results are preliminary until we file our Form 10-K with the Securities and Exchange Commission. On this morning's call is Lori Flees, our President and CEO; and Kevin Willis, our CFO. As shown in the accompanying presentation, any of our remarks today that are not statements of historical facts are forward-looking statements.

These forward-looking statements are based on current assumptions as of the date of this presentation and are subject to certain risks and uncertainties that may cause actual results to differ materially from such statements. Valvoline assumes no obligation to update any forward-looking statements unless required by law. In this presentation and in our remarks, we will be discussing our results on an adjusted non-GAAP basis, unless otherwise noted. A reconciliation of our GAAP to adjusted non-GAAP results and a discussion of management's use of non-GAAP and key business measures is included in the presentation appendix. I'll now turn it over to Lori to begin today's call.

Lori Flees: Thanks, Elizabeth, and thank you all for joining us this morning. 2026 is a milestone year for Valvoline. We delivered our 20th consecutive year of same-store sales growth, an achievement that few retailers can claim. That consistency has endured through a financial crisis, a global pandemic and a wide range of economic conditions. This year also marks our 40th year in retail services. And for the first time, we surpassed $4 billion in annual system-wide store sales. These milestones reflect the strength of what we've built and the durability of our business model.

Our growth track record is a credit to our team members and franchise partners who deliver the quick, easy and trusted service our guests count on every day. To everyone across our system, I want to thank you. We delivered strong results in fiscal 2026, and we did so while managing through a dynamic macro environment. Let's first look at what we saw in the fourth quarter. Starting with supply. Despite broad industry disruption, we maintained the product availability we needed to serve our guests throughout the quarter. Given our scale and strong strategic supplier relationship, we continue to feel good about our supply position.

As expected, the Group III base oil supply constraints drove a significant increase in finished lubricant costs in Q4. The company pricing actions we discussed on our last call were sufficient to cover the additional product costs we experienced during the quarter on a dollar basis. And our franchisees took additional pricing actions to offset their higher costs in the quarter. Outside of product costs, we remain disciplined in managing labor, store expenses and SG&A. Consumer behavior has been consistent. The demand for our services remains resilient. We continue to see transaction growth across the system. That reflects the essential nature of vehicle maintenance and the strength of our value proposition.

Even as customers become more value conscious, they understand the importance of maintaining their vehicles. Our customer retention improved year-over-year for each customer type, including our existing customers, new customers and fleet customers. Our customer satisfaction was also up. Net Promoter Scores are above 80, and we've earned an average 4.8 Google star rating across our network. We're encouraged by the strength of our existing customer relationships and our ability to keep guests coming back. Our premium lubricant mix continued to grow, and we've seen no changes in trade down behavior. Also, the rate at which customers added non-oil change services held stable year-over-year.

However, as we shared in August, we continue to see pockets of softness in growth, most notably at stores in lower-income areas where households are facing tighter budgets. This is largely attributable to slightly lower levels of new customer acquisition. On the network side, our growth was strong in the quarter with 61 additions, 33 company-operated locations and 28 franchise locations, which brings our network total to 2,517. As we approach the 1-year mark, we're pleased with the progress we're making on the Breeze integration. This quarter, we converted another 16 stores to the Valvoline brand, and we remain confident in the value this acquisition is expected to deliver.

With that, I'll turn it over to Kevin to walk through more detail on our fourth quarter and full year adjusted results.

John Willis: Thanks, Lori, and good morning, everyone. I'll start with the financial highlights for the fourth quarter, all of which were in line with our expectations. Net sales increased 22% to $555 million. This growth reflects momentum in the core business and the contribution from Breeze. Same-store sales growth for the quarter was 9.4%. Transaction growth was flat to slightly positive. Ticket delivered a majority of the comp. Net pricing, premiumization and NOCR were all positive contributors. Gross profit margin decreased 300 basis points year-over-year at the low end of the expected decline we shared on our last earnings call.

About 1/4 of the decline came from a temporary lag in passing the full cost increases through to franchisees, which was resolved in the quarter. Labor was slightly higher as a percent of net sales, although we saw labor leverage in our company-operated stores. SG&A as a percent of net sales improved 50 basis points year-over-year to 17.2%. That reflects the benefit of top line growth, along with continued cost discipline across the business. We continue to focus on improving operating leverage while supporting the growth of the business and navigating the macro environment. EBITDA increased 10% to $144 million, with adjusted EBITDA margin decreasing 280 basis points year-over-year to 25.9%.

EPS decreased to $0.42 per share, primarily due to higher interest expense related to the Breeze acquisition and a higher tax rate, which included state taxes tied in part to our network expansion. For the full year, we delivered strong results, meeting or exceeding our updated guidance. These results reflect solid execution throughout the year, including our ability to manage the significant product cost increases while delivering on our financial commitments. System-wide same-store sales grew 7.9% at the upper end of our guidance range. Across the system, we saw modest transaction growth while ticket drove the majority of the comp. We added 337 stores across the system. Net sales reached $2.1 billion, within our guidance range.

Adjusted EBITDA of $557 million was above the midpoint and adjusted EPS of $1.76 per share exceeded the top end. Capital expenditures were below the low end of the range at $228 million. This reflects the timing of store additions and conversions as well as lower store conversion costs. Free cash flow was $204 million, more than 4x our fiscal 2025 level. Breeze performed ahead of our expectations for the year with revenue and EBITDA contribution exceeding our outlook. The headwind to EBITDA margin was about 20 basis points, well below what we initially anticipated, reflecting operational improvements, early delivery of G&A synergies and the impact of early marketing and fleet initiatives.

We made meaningful progress to strengthen our balance sheet. We ended the year at 2.6x net debt to adjusted EBITDA from the combination of adjusted EBITDA growth during fiscal 2026 and debt reduction. Our recent leverage-neutral refinancing extended our debt maturity profile and enhanced our liquidity, leaving us with meaningful financial flexibility and no near-term debt maturities. We also increased the share repurchase authorization to $500 million and are committed to resume repurchases following this earnings release subject to market conditions. We made significant progress towards remediating the previously disclosed material weakness, and we expect to report in our upcoming Form 10-K that it has been remediated.

Overall, we exited fiscal 2026 with strong earnings, significantly improving cash generation and a healthy balance sheet, positioning us well as we enter fiscal 2027. With that, I'll turn it back to Lori to talk about our priorities for fiscal 2027.

Lori Flees: Thank you, Kevin. As we look ahead to fiscal 2027, we remain confident in the fundamentals of our business and our ability to execute in a dynamic environment. Our strategy is unchanged. We're focused on driving the full potential of the core business, delivering sustainable network growth and innovating as customer and vehicle needs evolve. So let me spend some time on our focus areas for fiscal 2027. Within the core business, our work is straightforward: drive transactions, grow ticket and improve margin. Our outlook assumes no change in the supply environment, stable product availability and continuation of elevated product costs. Our team will keep executing with discipline and staying focused on serving our customers.

We expect customer demand will remain resilient. While the fundamental need for preventative vehicle maintenance is unchanged, we are planning for an environment where consumers remain value-conscious. Therefore, we'll continue to adapt our approach to drive transaction growth as market conditions evolve. In 2027, we will launch our national advertising fund across the system, which we expect to increase brand awareness and improve marketing efficiency and driving more traffic to our stores. Regarding ticket, we'll be disciplined in our pricing actions. In the near term, we'll protect gross profit dollars while supporting long-term customer relationships. We have a long track record of managing price through changing cost environments, and we'll monitor consumer behavior and adjust as appropriate.

Beyond price, we expect premiumization to support ticket growth as the car parc evolves, and we'll continue to benefit from non-oil change service penetration opportunities. On margin, while the cost environment will continue to put pressure on our margin rate, we'll advance the margin improvement work already underway across the business, including G&A, labor management and store operating efficiencies. We expect these efforts to improve productivity as we scale and to deliver improved performance over the longer term. Network growth remains a priority across both our company-operated and franchise businesses. Our pipeline remains strong, and we are making progress on our commitment to lower new unit and conversion capital costs.

In fiscal 2027, we plan to add 190 to 220 new stores across the system with franchise locations representing roughly half of those additions. We'll complement that growth with the conversion of Breeze stores to the Valvoline brand. Looking at the evolving needs of our customers in the car parc, fleet continues to be an important growth opportunity for us. In fiscal 2026, fleet revenue saw high teens growth, driven from both higher ticket and healthy transaction growth. That transaction growth is coming from both our expanded store network and increased fleet penetration at existing stores. We expect to build on that momentum in fiscal 2027. We're confident in the fundamentals of the business and the opportunities ahead.

We've demonstrated our ability to execute through challenging conditions, and that discipline will remain important in the year ahead. I'll turn it back over to Kevin to walk through our financial outlook.

John Willis: Thanks, Lori. Our outlook calls for continued growth in fiscal 2027. We expect same-store sales growth of 5% to 7% on a full year basis. At the beginning of the year, we expect same-store sales to run at the high end or potentially above our range before moderating toward the low end as we lap pricing actions taken in fiscal 2026. Our guidance for net sales is $2.25 billion to $2.35 billion. Our adjusted EBITDA range is $595 million to $625 million with adjusted EPS of $1.85 to $2.05 per share. Capital expenditures are expected to be $245 million to $265 million. This outlook reflects a few key assumptions.

On the top line, we expect same-store sales transaction growth across the system to be roughly flat for the year. We assume product costs will remain elevated throughout the year and expect pricing adjustments to offset any further increases on a dollar basis. For the full year, gross margin deleverage is expected to be approximately 100 basis points with product cost pressure partially offset by labor leverage. In the first half, we expect margin deleverage to be approximately 200 to 250 basis points, moderating throughout the year. Below the gross profit line, we are planning for SG&A leverage. At the midpoint of the ranges, implied EBITDA margin is about 50 basis points lower compared to fiscal 2026.

Similar to prior years, we expect to deliver approximately 40% to 45% of adjusted EBITDA in the first half of the year. Our profit guidance reflects a range of outcomes for the year. The high end of the range incorporates improving consumer trends, leading to higher transaction growth and positive NOCR penetration growth. Moderating product costs would also be a benefit. The low end contemplates softness expanding into higher income households which could cause lower transaction growth and/or lower penetration of non-oil change services. Our capital allocation commitments remain the same: invest in the business, maintain a healthy balance sheet and return capital to shareholders.

We remain committed to the free cash flow target we shared at our investor update, 4 to 5x our fiscal 2025 level. Consistent with this, I'm pleased to confirm that subject to market conditions, we will resume share repurchases once our trading window opens following today's release. Returning capital to shareholders remains a priority and resuming repurchases reflects the strength of our balance sheet and our confidence in the cash and earnings generation of the business. I'll mention a couple of additional items for modeling purposes. Interest expense is projected to be $105 million to $110 million for the year, and we expect our adjusted tax rate to be approximately 26.5%, which is in line with fiscal 2026.

We'll continue to navigate a dynamic macro and cost environment in fiscal 2027. We'll remain focused on execution to deliver profitable growth. Lori Flees: Sure. I'll cover some of the elasticity and then, Kevin, you can cover the cadence question. It's a good question, Steve. I think you have to step back and remember that Valvoline competes on convenience, speed and trust. And we have to certainly watch our pricing, but it's not the first driver for consumers and for fleet customers on the brand that they choose. That said, whenever you take pricing increases and we look back at history, when we've taken pricing increases over 5%, you do expect some elasticity. But that's there. It's been very, very small in our historical events, and we would expect the same to be true.

When you raise price, there are a couple of things you look for right away as indicators. You look for trade downs, meaning customers whose OEM does not require or recommend full synthetic, but the customer is getting it anyway. Do they choose to trade down to something lower? Or do they change the number of services that they add on to the oil change? And that's the piece that we've been watching very closely. When we look at our retention rates, again, as I mentioned in the prepared remarks, our retention rates are up year-over-year. John Willis: I'll take that one, Max. In terms of the changes that we've made around how we price to franchisees, the concept is unchanged. As we talked about, we did get a little bit behind because the run-up in cost was faster than the pass-through, and we've changed that process so that we have very, very little lag. I would say, right now, it's very, very small.

Again, not necessarily a predictor of what may come, but we're happy about that. Stable product cost is kind of good for us all the way around. It's good for our franchise partners, too. And so we don't really expect any kind of meaningful timing difference in terms of what and when we pass through to the franchise partners to the extent that we see our cost go up or down. And so I think we feel really good about that.

We had a small lag in Q4, which we corrected with process change, and we feel good about that going forward. In terms of the $5 to $7 per oil change, there's really no difference. Lori Flees: Yes. So Max, we made a commitment of 100 to 200 basis points of improvement in EBITDA margins. I think last quarter, we sort of talked about prices don't generally come down as much as the input lag. So does the budget feel like it's rational?

Lori Flees: Yes. So far, as I mentioned in the prepared remarks, the consumer behavior has remained consistent despite the pricing increases. So when we look at our retention rate, when we look at our NOCR attach rate, et cetera, things are very stable. I think the question will be around -- I don't think we'll want to bring prices down. The question is, will we want to use some of the margin to drive more customer acquisition in the environment? That's going to drive more top line and more profit. So I think we'll always be weighing those things off and what's going to drive the long-term value of the business.

The reality is when we go out and acquire customers, the whole point is we get them back and we get them back repeatedly over time, and that becomes a flywheel to drive more earnings through the growth. So I don't -- based on everything we're seeing, we wouldn't see a need to pull back on price. We can drop that to margin without doing promotional activities or we could drive some promotional marketing spend to acquire more customers, which is going to drive more profit. So net-net, it's going to be a positive.

Bret Jordan: Okay. I guess my question is more like from a competitor standpoint, you're out-comping your peers pretty significantly. Do you see them using price as a tool to try to catch back up? Or will there be discounting in the broader market? That sounds like, no.

Lori Flees: Yes. I mean, I just remember that the oil change category is very fragmented. So there are very few competitors who, if they decided to do promotional impacts that would -- or promotional kind of activities that will impact our entire network. We see discount levels in Q4 were relatively consistent, and that was through the pricing cycles on a percentage basis, which is actually pretty typical in our industry. As dollars per ticket goes up, the discounting percentage typically stays relatively flat for a period of time and then you can optimize within. And some of that is because in the store, you'll have a customer react and the team wants to maintain that relationship.

And again, these are things that we have a long track record in managing. And we've factored that in, and that was factored into our Q4 number and guidance, and it's factored into our fiscal '27. We have heard of discounting activities, but we're not seeing anything major or broad across the network. I would say that we're pretty well positioned to take share in the supply environment. And to be honest, what we're hearing is others are not growing transactions, and we're still having very healthy transaction growth and even positive on a same-store sales basis. I'm not sure we're hearing that from our competitors.

Bret Jordan: Okay. And then non-oil change revenue, you said it was sort of flat year-over-year. Is there anything new on the non-oil change menu? Or is -- have you trotted anything else out? Or is it pretty much the same offerings?

Lori Flees: Yes. NOCR penetration rates are relatively stable. And that takes a lot of work from our team in really executing on the process and educating customers. The fact that so much of our business comes from repeat business, our customers actually know what to expect, and they've been educated on the needs of their vehicle. When we look at fleet penetration, our fleet penetration continues to grow, and they are less inclined to defer on any of those services. So all of those things really bode well. We have a few things that we're testing. In terms of -- we've had -- customers and franchise partners have already offered some premium filters. Oil Changers had that offering as well.

And so we have started to pilot offering premium filters for customers who again want to invest in taking care of the maintenance of their vehicles. That presents a very small upside. I wouldn't say it's all in our numbers. It's not meaningful. Obviously, if it is, we'll let you know, and we'll update our numbers accordingly. But overall, we continue to learn from Breeze. We continue to learn from our franchisees and listen to our customers, and we'll adapt some of our services as a result.

Operator: Your next question is from the line of Mike Albanese with StoneX.

Michael Albanese: Yes. Just another quick one here on pricing. I mean, I think you laid out your expectations pretty good, plan to kind of hold price and rebuild margins if oil costs come down. And historically, this model has been able to do that. I guess I just want to take it one level deeper here and just get some sense on what you would need to see that would make you kind of take pause or change your strategy and say, hold on, we need to give back some price and rebuild traffic? I mean, is it simply flipping negative? I think you said your midpoint on transactions next year is flat.

Is it going negative and staying negative for consecutive months or quarters? I mean, how do you think about what you would need to see to kind of shift gears?

Lori Flees: Yes. Good question. So really, we look at retention. And if we start to see any significant changes out of the ordinary on customer retention, that's when I think we have to look at our pricing and whether or not that's a driver or whether it's other issues. Again, our retention has improved year-over-year. The promotional side or how you discount, and we've been doing a lot of work on offering the right discount at the right time to the right potential customer.

And obviously, with new customers, when you are facing customers that feel very constrained from a spend perspective, it's typically not the time that they're going to try something new unless you give them something compelling, a compelling reason to do it. And that's the area where we have to continue to adapt and figure out the right approach to new customer acquisition in the environment and in those markets that have more value -- higher value-conscious consumer base.

Michael Albanese: So just to kind of frame that, I mean, it sounds like the first lever you would pull would be from a marketing standpoint or maybe an incentive standpoint, but less so about price. Is that fair?

Lori Flees: Yes, that has been the historical operating approach for us, but also in the industry.

Michael Albanese: Okay. All right. And then just a follow-up, quick one on Breeze here. Obviously, you said it's running in line with your expectations, if not better. I think when you first bought the 160 stores, they were doing about $1.1 million in average AUVs. Can we kind of maybe quantify this a little bit? I'd be curious as to maybe some of the earlier stores that you integrated, how that $1.1 million has grown or is tracking relative to the nearly $2 million kind of overall system average.

John Willis: I'll take that one, Mike. For the 28 stores that we've converted, it's still early days, but we are seeing positive momentum, and they're performing at or slightly above where we would expect them to be based upon what we would normally do for stores that we acquire. Just as a reminder, we'll buy 30 to 40 Quick Lubes per year and convert those to the Valvoline brand and then they start climbing the curve. And generally, those stores do reach the AUV that we would expect and that we enjoy in our overall store base. And it's really a combination of things. Number one, there's a marketing playbook that we implement that helps drive that.

It brings in new customers and really drives the transaction growth in those stores. And number two, the Valvoline brand is very strong. It's well recognized. That tends to bring in traffic as well. And number three, we have a very long-standing approach to how we train our teams and how they execute in the store with our customers that's very, very consistent, which not only drives traffic on the front end, but also keeps those customers coming back and really drives the very high retention rate that we enjoy. All of those things in combination help these stores and should help the Breeze stores climb the curve.

They are very, very similar to many stores that we would acquire with the exception that, frankly, they are generally better operated than many of the stores we would acquire on a onesie or twosie basis throughout the course of the year. And so they already have a very strong base from which to grow. They just need the Valvoline playbook, training, systems and brand to actually make that come to fruition over the course of time as they mature.

Michael Albanese: That's great. Is it generally a linear progression?

John Willis: So generally, year 1, you'll see a little bit of a dip as we invest in the store.

Lori Flees: In profit. But in sales, it goes up.

John Willis: In profit. That's right. Sales will go up. Profit will go down a little bit because we're investing in the team, we're investing in marketing, et cetera. But then it becomes pretty linear from there in terms of the maturity curve after...

Lori Flees: It's about 3 years of linear and then you'll get improvements in year 4 and 5, but to a lesser extent. It's a typical ramp is what we're expecting for our new -- our store conversions.

Operator: Your next question is from the line of Kevin Condon with Baird.

Kevin Condon: A bit of a follow-up to the prior question. But in fiscal 2027, as some of those Breeze stores start entering the comp equation, is that a tailwind to same-store sales just as those small stores grow and benefit from the rebranding? Or I guess given your comments on the EBITDA drag being less in 2026 and some of those integration improvements hitting earlier, is the comp uplift from the maturation of that store maybe less than a typical curve?

John Willis: Yes. So as a reminder, we converted 28 stores in fiscal '26. We would expect those stores to enter the comp in our Q2. So typically, it's a year after acquisition that a store would enter the comp. And so that would imply the beginning of our Q2, the March quarter. And again, as a reminder, it's 28 stores on a base of 2,500-plus stores. So while we would expect it to be a tailwind, it will be very, very small at this point. But we do expect it -- those stores to contribute.

Lori Flees: Yes. It will be small and growing over the time we integrate. So as we integrate -- continue to integrate stores in '27 and they enter the comp, it will obviously build over time, but it will be fairly small at the start of the year.

Kevin Condon: Got you. So it's a year after you convert the stores to the Valvoline brand that they start contributing to that comp equation. So it's...

John Willis: No. The way to think about it is we acquired the Breeze stores on December 1 of last year. And so December 1 of this year will mark 1 year that we've owned them. And we converted 28 stores in fiscal -- during fiscal '26. And so those 28 stores will enter the comp basically at the beginning of our Q2, which is the March quarter. So come January, those stores will be part of the comp because we will have owned them for a year by then. And so that's the way to think about that.

Lori Flees: And our same-store sales comp guidance is based on Valvoline Instant Oil Change stores in the United States. It has excluded Canada, and it will exclude the Oil Changers branded stores. So it'll just be Valvoline branded stores in the U.S. and only stores that we've owned for 1 year or more. So consistent with the definition that we switched to.

Operator: Your next question is from the line of Scott Stember with ROTH Capital Partners.

Scott Stember: Trying to get a sense of some of the areas that you saw in certain markets weakness, trying to get a sense of a cadence if we've seen things get worse as the quarter progressed. Just trying to get a sense of how sensitive these customers in the -- I guess, in the lower income markets are as they really feel the full brunt of these increased prices for an oil change.

Lori Flees: Yes. I don't think it's so much around the price change of oil changes. I think these are customers that broadly are feeling constraints from the macro inflationary gas prices, et cetera. And in Q4, we also recognized that people are making a variety of choices, particularly around the holidays. And so we've also factored in the fact that in times where people are making trade-offs, they may defer until next quarter services that they are due if they're not finite. So we anticipate customers in lower income demographics to be making certain trade-offs in Q4. But the reality is they are not trading off maintenance. The behavior -- customers understand the importance of maintaining their vehicles.

And part of this is driven by the cost that it would be to upgrade their vehicle. So buying a new used vehicle, the cost of car loans, et cetera, is very prohibitive versus where many people are today or the cost of major repairs. So it's a timing issue for some of these customers, not necessarily not going to do it at all. And so I don't think we see this getting worse for an extended period of time. Obviously, if the economy, something that we're not forecasting, and the pressure becomes broader, we don't see our customers' behavior changing all that much. And our numbers based on small changes are not highly sensitive.

At a company of our size, that actually works in our favor.

Scott Stember: Got it. And then just last question, going back to Breeze integration and synergies. One of the synergies that we've talked about is they're adopting fleet services and the non-oil change NOCR. Can you just talk about how those trends are going, particularly as things are within the market have tightened up?

Lori Flees: Yes. Great question. We integrated our fleet teams probably about 4 months ago. We fully integrated them in this quarter. So our fleet team now is working on behalf of the entire network. And we have sort of in our national fleet accounts, we've included Breeze sites in all of the accounts that we could do so given the way that they bill and that those contracts are arranged. So we're seeing some good momentum, though we're in early days around the Breeze stores.

It's much easier once we convert them, but we have done quite a number of things to try to get some of that growth earlier, and that's contributed to some of the Breeze momentum that we saw in the year. And on NOCR -- sorry. I would just say on NOCR, they did a pretty good job. We've been working across services that Valvoline offers that they don't, services that they offered that we didn't on the Valvoline side. In some cases, we are picking up some offerings, as I mentioned, premium filters as an option for our guests. That's something that some of our franchise partners have already done and Oil Changers did.

So we're getting the -- we'll get the benefit of that across our system. And then on the Oil Changers side, we're trying to work through, and I think we've decided not to add services into Oil Changers until we convert them. And part of that is the systems lift that you have to make in order to add those things into your offering. So I think we're just trying to pace is the juice worth the squeeze or do we wait until we convert the store. And I think we've decided that adding things to the Oil Changers stores will be probably to a minimum until we convert the stores.

Operator: Your next question is from the line of David Lantz with Wells Fargo.

David Lantz: So you mentioned fleet penetration continues to grow and that revenues for that segment grew high teens in fiscal '26. So curious if you can size that contribution to the 7.9% comp for the year and what you're embedding for the '27 outlook?

John Willis: David, as you look at the fleet business, it's still -- it's growing very well. We're incredibly pleased with how that is progressing. But as a reminder, it still represents under 10% of total system-wide sales. Again, growing very quickly, but still relatively small, which gives us a lot of encouragement around the opportunity now and into the future as we continue to grow that business. But I think the way to think about it is it's growing faster than the system, but it's still less than 10% of the system. And so it will be a contributor, but it's still relatively small in terms of the percentage of contribution.

David Lantz: Got it. That's helpful. And then just two more quick ones. Can you talk about the cadence of openings throughout the year in 2027 as well as the cadence of comps through fiscal Q4 and anything that you're seeing on a quarter-to-date basis?

John Willis: So in terms of the cadence of comps, currently, we're enjoying obviously price that's ahead of where we were last year and will be for the first half of the year. So we would expect the comp to be at or even initially above the high end of the 5% to 7% outlook range that we've given for the first half of the year and then moderating as we start to lap the pricing that both we and our franchise partners took in Q3, primarily for us in Q4 more so for them, so that -- which would take us more to the, call it, the low end of that range probably for the second half of the year.

Your other question was around the cadence of store openings?

Lori Flees: So I think we have -- there's a seasonality to the way that new store openings happen. We typically, in Q4, because of the timing of summer and construction as well as the timing of M&A that's going to hit by the end of the year, we typically have a pretty good Q4, then it goes lighter, and we tend to be very back-end loaded. And we've tried hard to pull that forward and be less back-end loaded. But the reality is construction cycles and M&A don't always align up well with that.

So I think you should think about the cadence to be consistent if you look back over the last 3 years with kind of where we've been on average.

Operator: Your next question is from the line of John Babcock with Barclays.

John Babcock: I guess just first on product costs. I want to get a little clarity here. So I think you're targeting up 60% by the end of the quarter. If you could just confirm that's where you were? And then also, how much are costs up now relative to the same starting benchmark from, I think, probably the end of the last quarter?

John Willis: So in the quarter, we basically -- it came in as expected. Product -- finished lubricant costs were up about 60% and to be very clear, the pricing that we have taken has covered that cost increase. And so we feel very good about where we are there. I would say for the last few weeks, product cost has been pretty stable. We've seen some upward movement in the base oil index for Group III. But to be clear, the base oil index and the spot market are still very, very disconnected. And we haven't seen a lot of change from a spot market perspective. But yes, and we're happy about that. Less volatility is good.

Obviously, we're looking forward to seeing costs go the other way once the supply chain frees up over the course of time. But for now, product costs have been very stable for the last few weeks, and that's a good thing.

John Babcock: Okay. So it's fair to say that costs are basically up a little bit from the end of the quarter, but not up significantly.

John Willis: They're really not up versus the end of the quarter. It's been pretty stable for the last, call it, 4 to 6 weeks.

Lori Flees: Relative to the average for the quarter, they're slightly up, but where we exited...

Lori Flees: We're seeing a lot of stability. And we're not expecting any significant changes on the supply side, both to the positive or the negative. But we certainly will adapt if there are changes, and we're ready for that.

John Babcock: Okay. And then next question, I know like fuel prices don't always tend to fully recover back for whatever reason. I'm just wondering -- and I know you don't have a lot of precedence in terms of the market and like where it is today. So maybe this is a bad question. But just generally, do base oil costs tend to fully recover when crude oil costs come down? Or what sort of movement do you tend to see?

John Willis: I would say over the course of time, that will happen. But to be clear, the base oil market and the crude oil market don't necessarily run in tandem. For a long time, they did. But over the past couple of years, even well before what we're in right now, started to unfold, those 2 things had disconnected themselves. Again, eventually, and over the course of time, it will match up. But as you look at base oil, it's a supply-demand phenomenon really. And right now, we're still tight, especially in Group III.

And even as you see crude oil futures and gasoline prices, which do tend to track pretty quickly and pretty closely, as you've seen those things change, again, the base oil market really hasn't changed. And that's all about supply and demand, especially in Group III, which remains tight.

Lori Flees: And for now, it's not just about oil flow, it's also about refining capacity. And some of the refining capacity is shut down because of the lack of supply, and it will take time for that to get back up and running once supply lines are fully loaded. So I think that's also part of the disconnect that we're planning for. So we're taking the approach in FY '27 that the environment remains the same as it relates to supply and cost. And I think overall, we're ready to execute within that and deliver really good profitable growth.

John Babcock: All right. That's very helpful. One last one, if you don't mind me squeezing this in. Just obviously, you had like really good cost control during the quarter, and it sounds like you're continuing to target cost across the company. Do you have any specific cost reduction targets that you have for the year? Or maybe you prefer not to go down that route?

John Willis: Yes. We prefer to not go down that path. I mean, we're continuing to work internally with the team to find efficiencies in the system and to execute against those. But we don't have any targets that we've talked about publicly. It's really all about executing well, continuing to find those efficiencies and then execute on those.

Operator: Your final question is from the line of Steven Zaccone with Citi.

Steven Zaccone: Great. It's very confusing with the base oil cost environment. I imagine it's very difficult to manage this as well. But I guess a simple question here, right? If we think about base oil costs kind of staying the same, right, and being stable, at what point would the gross margin rate get back to a level you were before we had all this disruption?

John Willis: Yes. I think the way we're looking at it right now, as we've said in our outlook, we're expecting the total gross margin compression for the full year of 100 basis points. And the implication is, as we said, approximately 200 to 250 basis points of compression kind of in the first half of the year, and you could infer that Q1 would be at the high end of that. And over the course of the year, we expect that to moderate.

So I would say all things being equal, if they ever are, all things being equal, we would expect to close that gap probably towards the end of the fiscal year, most likely based on where our outlook is.

Steven Zaccone: Okay. That's very helpful. Final one I had was just on the national advertising campaign. So I guess you're having a little bit of an expansion this year in terms of the fund. So maybe just talk about would that result in a comp lift? Like how do you think through that?

Lori Flees: Yes. Great question. So on the company side, in '26, we talked about flipping some of our local brand spend to national advertising, and we did see efficiency benefits on that, particularly in the fourth quarter. In FY '27, our entire system will start our national advertising fund, which will -- when you look at our financials, it will show that advertising costs are up at a greater rate, but this is more franchise funds flowing through and us spending them. So it's going to look a little odd on the P&L, but it is really just the franchise contributing to an advertising fund that we manage on behalf of the system.

And that increase in spend is going to drive more impressions at a much lower cost, which then drives better efficiency in the lower funnel. And what does that mean lower funnel? We get more people coming to search for Valvoline, come to Valvoline owned sites to learn more about our product and come to our stores where we're not paying for that traffic. And so that's going to allow us to use the money more wisely to actually attract and acquire more new customers into the system.

And that's part of the reason why when Kevin talked about total growth in transactions, we expect total growth in transactions to be the same or higher in this fiscal year than '26. And some of that is driven by what we believe will happen in the national ad fund. Now obviously, we will look to overperform, but we're early on in this. And so as we learn, we'll obviously update our expectations.

Operator: We have reached the end of the Q&A. This concludes today's call. Thank you for attending. You may now disconnect.
