Dreaming of Buying a Home Abroad? These 10 Countries Make It Shockingly Simple

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you’ve always wanted to live out your expat fantasies, start here.

Over the last few years, many Americans have daydreamed about life abroad. Moving internationally can often feel like a pipe dream, but with the right planning, it’s possible. However, lengthy visa processes, daunting language barriers, and a difficult-to-navigate legal system can make the dream feel more like a nightmare.

Certain countries, like Australia, Canada, and New Zealand, have bans on foreign buyers. Others, like Thailand, Cuba, and Vietnam, don’t allow outsiders to own property at all. On the other end of the spectrum are countries actively trying to attract foreign buyers, offering enviable incentives like affordable home prices, a smoother customs process, and an easier-to-navigate work-visa approval process.

Choosing the right location can make moving abroad much less intimidating. Ahead, we’re breaking down the ten best countries for Americans looking to buy property abroad. Whether you’re dreaming of relocating or searching for your perfect vacation home, these countries make buying as easy as possible and offer gorgeous landscapes too.

If you’ve ever dreamed of living among medieval architecture and rolling mountain ranges, Georgia might be the key to unlocking your fantasy. A bridge between Eastern Europe and Western Asia, the transcontinental country borders Russia, Turkey, Armenia, and the Black Sea, offering both subtropical and high-altitude climates. Thankfully, the real estate landscape is much less complex, with few legal or bureaucratic hurdles for American buyers, no residency or visa required (for up to a year), and remarkably fast processing times. In fact, the only major restriction in the Georgia market is that foreigners cannot purchase or own agricultural land, a nod to the importance of the local, small-scale farming cultures peppered across the country’s regions.

Uruguay’s buying process could not be more straightforward. South America’s second-smallest sovereign country requires no citizenship, visa, or prior approvals. Under Uruguayan law, foreign buyers are given the same rights as citizens and enjoy the benefits of free capital flow. Locals are just as, if not more, tourist-friendly. The country is known for its hospitality, laid-back café culture, and stunning coastal landscapes.

Instead of retiring to Florida, consider this balmy, beautiful Caribbean country—just a four-hour direct flight from the New York area. Like everything else on the island, the real estate laws are relatively relaxed, making buying a house easy-breezy; and you can buy and own property without obtaining residency status or special permissions, as is the case in many other Caribbean islands. “Foreigners can own property outright in the Dominican Republic, with legal protections for foreign investors,” says Mauricio Umansky, CEO and founder of The Agency. One important consideration is financing: You can certainly get a mortgage in the DR, but it may not be quite as cushy as what you can get in the U.S.—so pay cash if possible.

Great news if you’ve been wanting a more provincial life: Americans face zero restrictions when buying homes in France. The open market allows for statesiders to buy houses, apartments, or plots of land in the city, countryside, and coastal towns in person or remotely without needing citizenship, residency, or even a local visa. However, a purchase does not grant residency, so if you’re hoping to make France your permanent home, you’ll want to apply for a separate long-stay visa and note the Schengen 90-out-of-180-day rule. While buying may be simple, the finances are anything but. Closing fees, including notary fees, typically add seven to ten percent to your purchase. Mortgage rates are also incredibly hard to secure for U.S.-based buyers, thanks to strict debt-to-income caps and FATCA compliance. To simplify the process, many buyers opt to put 20 percent to 40 percent down in cash.

Perhaps the most American-friendly market, Panama boasts a large English-speaking market, virtually no restrictions on foreign property ownership, and uses the U.S. dollar as legal tender in a vastly affordable market. The Central American nation also offers residency programs like the Friendly Nations Visa, clearing the way legally for residency by investment. In addition to an efficient legal system and reliable market, Panama boasts breathtaking beaches, lush rainforests, and exceptional wildlife.

If you’re hoping for a home in the coffee valleys of Colombia, you’ll need to hire a real estate lawyer before you start scouting for mountain chalets, coffee triangle treehouses, and rainforest retreats. The Caribbean country opts out of standardized agent licensing systems, making legal aid essential to verify title and freedom certificates. While citizenship isn’t required, you will need to secure a tourist visa and Colombian Tax ID to complete your purchase and register for property taxes.

The buying process in Greece is lengthy, though it is streamlined, with a clear checklist for American buyers to find success in the market. Hiring a local lawyer or notary is a great first step after scouting properties. Not only are they familiar with title checks and contracts, but they can also navigate the intricacies that come with buying in designated border areas or near military zones, which require special clearance for non–EU buyers. While the most popular areas for American buyers, like Athens and Crete, don’t come with heavy legal intricacies, buying any property in Greece will mean securing a Greek Tax Number—a small price to pay for cities with such rich histories and breathtaking seaside views.

The people in this popular Western European coastal country are famously friendly and inviting, and that extends to real estate. According to Samira Easton, a certified international property specialist with Premier Sotheby’s International Realty, Portugal offers a Golden Visa Program, which makes it simple to buy property and gain residency; clear real estate laws; and a well-established network of English-speaking real estate agents, lawyers, and notaries to help you during the process.

Belize is home to one of the most vibrant, tourist-friendly cultures in Central America, mixing Mayan, Hispanic, Asian, and Creole cultures in its dense rainforests and rolling low plains. Buying here is as easy as it can get, as outright ownership applies to foreigners, and a British-inspired legal system abolishes language barriers and makes contracts easier to understand. Even more, there is no capital gains tax in Belize, and property taxes trend low. The country also boasts vibrant marine life and is home to the world’s second-largest barrier reef.

Spain may have ended its investor visa program in 2025, but the real estate market is still remarkably easy for Americans to navigate. The country currently imposes no restrictions on foreign or nonresident property ownership, but there are a few things you’ll need to procure before you buy, like a Foreigner Identification Number, which is mandatory for taxes. Despite the minor hurdles, the market is booming both on the ground and online. “There are also numerous online resources and property portals available, so it’s easy to find listings and information,” Easton says. This is a boon for those who aren’t able to make the trip over to Europe to house shop in person.Note that buying property does not grant you residency or bypass the country’s 90-day Schengen limit. If you’re hoping to turn your Spanish holiday into your everyday, you’ll have to apply for a separate visa.

You can get the iPhone Air with 256GB of storage for free, as long as it’s activated on a qualifying plan for 36 months. I’ve gone through all the steps and found that this deal works with AT&T’s Value 2.0 plan, which costs $50/month and comes with unlimited talk, text, and 5GB of high-speed data.

Even though T-Mobile’s offer isn’t as generous, you can still score a free iPhone Air when you activate it on a new line, port your old number, and sign up for a T-Mix plan with three “Picks” perks. Alternatively, you can sign up for a new line and trade in an eligible phone in any condition.

RedPocket Mobile’s giving an instant $100 discount on the iPhone Air, which pairs nicely with the carrier’s generous $10/month cell phone plans — including its Premium plan, which is discounted to $10/month for Black Friday and comes with 50GB of data.

With no standard iPhone 18 expected until early 2027, the iPhone 17 is still the go-to phone for anyone that isn't interested in a Pro model.

You can score an iPhone 17 at AT&T for free by signing up for a Extra 2.0 plan or higher, along with an eligible trade-in of an older device with a value of at least $130.

Similarly, T-Mobile's giving away the iPhone 17 for free, just as long as you sign up for a four pick T-Mix plan. Alternatively, you can also opt for a two-pick plan by taking out a new line and either porting your number from another carrier or trading in an eligible smartphone in any condition.

If you don’t want the hassle of trade-ins, check out Boost Mobile’s iPhone 17 Black Friday deal, which shaves $300 off the cost of the phone. You simply need to activate it on Boost Mobile’s $60/month Unlimited Premium plan.

If you’re looking to spend the least amount of money possible, the option to look at is the iPhone 17e. While it technically has the lowest starting cost in the lineup, Black Friday deals shave even more off the sticker price.

Yes, the iPhone 17e is clearly free at T-Mobile when you match the right requirements. In order to get it, you need to sign up for a three-pick T-Mix plan, or if you prefer, you can sign up for a one-pick plan with a new line and choose between trading in an old device or porting your old number from a different carrier.

iPhone 17e: $0.43/month w/AT&T Installment PlanRather than going through the hassle of meeting specific terms, you can get a low-cost iPhone 17e courtesy of AT&T. The carrier's offering it for as little as $0.43 a month. No that's not a typo, and the catch is you need to open a new line on an eligible plan.

You can get $350 off instantly on the iPhone 17e courtesy of Boost Mobile. There's no quirky requirements either, like a trade-in. Instead, the only thing you need to do is activate it for 2 months with the carrier's $60/month Unlimited Premium plan.

10 Details That Show GTA 6 Deserves Game of the Year

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Aden Carter is a Contributor at DualShockers who has been covering games professionally since 2020. He specializes in lists and guides, with a focus on RPGs, horror games, survival horror, Soulslikes, cozy/sim games, and action-adventure titles. Before joining DualShockers, Aden contributed to GameRant, XDA, Gamepur, I

Grand Theft Auto 6 is now available to the public, but the controversial part doesn’t have anything to do with the game itself, but rather, what the game has been nominated for.

The nominees for Game of the Year at The Game Awards have officially been announced, and before GTA 6 was available to the public, it was placed as a nominee. In fact, it was nominated for more than just Game of the Year.

This has obviously bothered many gamers, but there is a reason why GTA 6 is on this list of nominees. Let’s break down why this long-awaited title deserves Game of the Year.

Rockstar is known for adding some great music to games. GTA 5 has a pretty great soundtrack, with it featuring numerous artists. Because of that, it was no surprise that Rockstar started announcing various artists for GTA 6.

The radio is stacked with some amazing musicians who took the time to write new songs specifically for the game. With so much talent, it is difficult not to get lost in the beat, so long as you are someone who enjoys each artist’s music.

In the end, users recognize that this talent isn’t just for show, and the music in GTA 6 fits the vibe of the crime-forward series, making it feel more alive.

People have been waiting 13 years to get their hands on GTA 6. Ever since Grand Theft Auto 5 was released, people have been asking for the next game in the series. Well, over the course of those 13 years, Rockstar has been working hard.

The team has been busting their butts to develop GTA 6 and make it as big as possible. They have talked about how they created new systems, enhanced older ones, and added a ton of detail into their newest game.

With this much effort put in, there is no denying that the team deserves some recognition, and just being nominated shows that people care.

For a long time, The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time held the record for being the highest rated game of all time with a 99 on MetaCritic. Now that reviews for GTA 6 are out, it shares the same score as Ocarina of Time.

With that being said, there were 22 professional reviews for Ocarina of Time, and over 150 for GTA 6, so GTA 6 has a more accurate representation from multiple parties, putting it above Ocarina of Time.

That isn’t a fluke. You can look at a few review scores, see a 10/10 and write it off, but when there are over 100 different people giving it the same score or something similar, that isn’t a coincidence.

The reviews for Grand Theft Auto 6 are in and it's now tied for the best-reviewed video game of all time, with Nintendo's greatest game matching it.

There are many people, who, when they purchase a game, are in it for the visual aspect. There is nothing wrong with this, and with the advancement of graphics engines, it has become commonplace for games to try and look lifelike.

Grand Theft Auto 6 has some of the best visuals of any game that has been released this year, and even in the last decade. The developers at Rockstar spend a lot of time trying to make every shot look as cinematic as possible.

The result of this, is one of the most beautiful games to come out in a very long time, and it will hold up for many years to come. The only slight downside is the 30fps, but that is a small price to pay for this level of beauty.

Rockstar has spent ages working on GTA 6, and a big part of that work has gone into making the world feel more alive rather than a sandbox that you can run and gun in like it was in GTA 5. Previous titles didn’t respect you being a character in the world, but rather let you do whatever you wanted.

NPCs have received a major overhaul in GTA 6, reacting more to you being in the world, and turning the game into a major life sim where people will get angry with you if you pull a gun, run away in fear, and much more.

In addition to this, there are tons of activities to take part in, so much so that you can ignore the main quest for ages and still have plenty to do. You definitely won’t get bored with this game.

Instead of focusing on fleshing out three individual characters, this time around Rockstar decided to focus on creating a couple. Their names are Jason and Lucia, and their story is powerful, and they are far more fleshed out than characters in the previous game.

Jason and Lucia are madly in love, and they will do anything to stand by one another. The Bonnie and Clyde dynamic fits the location of Vice City very well, and it is a story that many didn’t think would work at first.

This type of emphasis on building a relationship is something that we haven’t seen in a while, and Rockstar made it work very well, meaning it is a game that will be remembered for a long time.

GTA VI will be one of the biggest video game releases in our lifetimes, and it will be talked about for years to come.

Rockstar is known for making some incredible characters, and GTA 6 is no exception to this. From the beginning to the end, there are numerous characters that you meet that have a lasting effect on the story and some that are only there for a moment but leave an impression.

Think about your time with GTA 5 and how it was fun interacting with the game’s characters. They made you laugh, and they certainly are remembered.

Well, GTA 6 has that and then some, with a cast of side characters that each have a standout personality that will make you remember them for some time to come. It shows that the devs knew what they were doing when creating the world of Leonida.

If there is something that the Rockstar devs understand it is comedy. There was so much of it baked into GTA 5 that it was difficult to avoid. Now that same energy has been carried over to GTA 6 and it is fantastic.

If you are a fan of satire then be prepared to see it everywhere in GTA 6 because nearly everything is a satirical take on a company, person, or event.

Rockstar devs are masters of comedic timing and satire, and GTA 6 proves it. It is a game that will have you chuckling at the references and laughing at the absurdity. It hits the funny bone and there is no denying that that plays a big role in why it is so good.

Something that I like in games is when you can edit your character in some way. I like to make my own character or dress up existing characters in fun outfits, and GTA 6 has that and then some.

Similar to previous games in the series, there are numerous outfits that you can purchase for your characters, which is a dream for those who like to customize their look. On top of that, you can edit your cars, and you can also edit your appearance through working out.

That’s right. The game is detailed enough that Lucia and Jason will gain weight, their hair will grow long, and more. You can spend time truly focusing on your character’s appearance, and that is something I want out of my game of the year.

Let’s be honest, there are many games that are released that are buggy messes. For instance, massive games like Crimson Desert and Assassin’s Creed Shadows had some massive glitches that hurt the games when they were first released.

It took some rather large patches to fix them, and even then there were still issues. GTA 6 is one of the few games that have been released that has little to no issues with it, and in 2026, that is a massive feat, especially for a game with the the scope of GTA 6.

The lack of issues with the game at launch with how big it is makes it a much more enticing pick for Game of the Year.

Grand Theft Auto 6 delivers an enormous open world, a gripping story, a vibrant Vice City and an astonishing range of activities.

Jamie and Corinne Foxx Developing Horror Series ‘Buried’ as Foxxhole Productions Extends First-Look Deal at Fox Entertainment Studios (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Fox Entertainment Studios has extended its first-look agreement with Foxxhole Productions, the company led by Corinne Foxx and Jamie Foxx, to 2028.

Jamie Foxx signed the first-look deal last year; terms of the extension specify that he will serve as an executive producer and content originator for scripted programming spanning comedy, drama and animation, with Fox Entertainment Studios retaining first-look rights. The agreement also gives the studio exclusive rights to Foxx’s unscripted projects. Related Stories Queen Latifah, Bone Thugs-N-Harmony, MC Lyte and More to Lead 2027 Rock the Bells Cruise

The Academy Award-winning actor, comedian and producer’s relationship with the Fox network dates back to 1991, when he landed his breakout role on the sketch comedy series “In Living Color.” That success led to his eponymous sitcom, “The Jamie Foxx Show,” which ran for five seasons.

Under the pact, Foxxhole Productions and Fox will develop the horror series “Buried,” with “The Conjuring” creators Chad and Carey Hayes and Shelley Meals (“It: Welcome to Derry”).

“We’re thrilled to continue our partnership with Fox Entertainment Studios and to keep building together across both scripted and unscripted,” Foxxhole Productions president Corinne Foxx said in a statement. “Foxxhole Productions is focused on bold, character-driven projects that are commercial, entertaining and often center Black voices and experiences. ‘Buried’ is the perfect example of that. We’re excited to be working with Shelley Meals alongside masters of horror Chad and Carey Hayes, who together are the perfect team to make this story as emotionally resonant as it is haunting.”

“Buried” is inspired by the true story of the residents of a Crosby, Texas subdivision who reported experiencing hauntings before discovering their new homes were built atop Black Hope Cemetery, a long-forgotten burial ground for a Freedmen’s Town settlement. “The reported supernatural phenomena drew national attention, and some residents say paranormal events continue today,” a release announcing the series notes.

The Hayes Brothers will serve as executive producers on “Buried” alongside Jamie and Corinne Foxx, who will executive produce through Foxxhole Productions, with Christina Porter, VP of scripted development, overseeing development. Meals will serve as showrunner and executive producer on the project.

A Pop-Star Acting Newbie, the Afterlife and “Dirty Energy”: The Making of ‘La Bola Negra’

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Directing duo Javier Ambrossi and Javier Calvo stretch a love story across the Spanish Civil War, three generations and.the great beyond.

Javier Calvo was traveling with his filmmaking partner, Javier Ambrossi, when he read Alberto Conejero’s play La Piedra Oscura (The Dark Stone) and the idea for La Bola Negra (The Black Ball) took hold. Conejero’s drama, set in a military hospital during the Spanish Civil War, imagines the last night of Rafael Rodríguez Rapún — a Republican lieutenant and the real-life lover of playwright Federico García Lorca — in conversation with Sebastián, the young Nationalist soldier who is to execute him at dawn.

“I imagined this little gay boy having to leave his town, all this tragedy, and how this happened to my country only 90 years ago, and having a gay character in the civil war,” says Calvo. “We never talk about that. Where were we? What happened to us? It hit me, and I started to cry. I told Javi, ‘I think we have to make this.’ ” Related Stories Movies 'Obsession' Breakout Inde Navarrette to Receive Spotlight Tribute at Gotham Film Awards (Exclusive) Movies The Free World Award Is Made From Weapons Metal. The First Winner: Iraq's Oscar Entry

The film, co-written with Conejero, braids three timelines. In 1932 Granada, Carlos (Milo Quifes), a young upper-class Andalusian, is blackballed from an exclusive casino because he’s gay — the premise of Lorca’s unfinished play La Bola Negra, of which only four pages survive. In 1937, Sebastián (singer- songwriter Guitarricadelafuente) keeps watch over Rafael (Miguel Bernardeau). And in 2017 Madrid, Alberto (Carlos González) learns that he is Sebastián’s grandson. Sebastián has left him the rest of Lorca’s play — pages the film imagines into being — which he had promised Rafael he would save. Those pages — verified by Glenn Close, who plays a Spanish-speaking American scholar named Isabelle Durand — lead Alberto and his relatives to discover that Sebastián spent most of his life secretly living with another man.

“This is a movie about what we pass on to the next generation,” Ambrossi says. “It’s about inheritance.”

The co-directors, known as Los Javis among their collaborators because of their shared first names, reassembled their usual crafts team to begin shaping each timeline: cinematographer Gris Jordana, editor Alberto Gutiérrez, production designer Roger Bellés, costume designer Ana López Cobos, composer Raül Refree and casting directors Eva Leira and Yolanda Serrano.

The directors were already keen on Guitarricadelafuente and Bernardeau for their respective roles, as well as González for the part of Alberto, which Ambrossi calls “a creation.”

“He’s so comedic and flamboyant, but we wanted to give him a role where he can prove the actor that he is because sometimes queer people are given the same [stereotypical] roles,” Ambrossi says.

Quifes, who like Guitarricadelafuente makes his screen debut in the film, was found through an open casting call. “He has a sense of melancholy about him, but it’s not about hopelessness; there’s a sense of hope and beauty,” says Leira. “And he has such a beautiful gaze. It’s a gaze that comes from ancient times.”

Los Javis also knew they wanted to shoot in 35mm from the outset, which aligned with DP Jordana’s approach. “You don’t have to fight the format to get images,” she says of 35mm film. “If you use the right light direction, the rest just comes together. It’s like magic.”

Light also drove the design of the opulent casino in Granada, the only set built from scratch. Everywhere else, the production restored existing buildings, hiring local artisans who still work with prewar methods. “At first glance, it looked like something crazy and unnecessary when we built it in the middle of Spain, but when you open the windows, you see Spain, not a green screen,” says production designer Bellés, who commissioned artist Ignasi Monreal to paint the mural on the ceiling in the style of Joaquín Sorolla, “one of the great painters of Spanish light,” he explains.

The windows were made of sugar glass so that they could shatter safely when the casino explodes near the end of the film after being overtaken by a mob as Carlos makes a final case for admission. The remaining structure was then filled with fake snow as the scene transitions to Carlos lying on a mountainside, realizing he has died from the bomb’s explosion. The shot, captured at an altitude of 3,000 meters (more than 9,800 feet high) in the snowy region of the Sierra Nevadas, brought to life elements described in Lorca’s poem — like the skiers on the slopes — which also inspired Guitarricadelafuente’s original song, “La Nieve (The Snow),” that plays in the background.

“With Federico García Lorca, there’s a lot of powerful imagery and symbolism, like in the beginning of the film, the horse running past Sebastián is a representation of untamable desire,” Guitarricadelafuente explains. “ ‘La Nieve’ symbolized the end of a journey and approaching death. I had to leave the emotional baggage of Sebastián to fully understand what he was carrying to be able to write that.”

Refree took a hands-on approach with Penélope Cruz, who portrays Nené Romero, a cupletista cabaret singer. “She told me, ‘I’m not a singer,’ but as soon as I heard her singing … I fell in love with her singing,” he says. “The problem was that she hadn’t created the character yet.”

Over the course of several days, Refree went to Cruz’s home for rehearsals, and she began to craft the persona of Nené, who serves as a portal to a world in which Sebastián can be free to be his true self when he sees her perform “Las Tardes del Ritz (Afternoons at the Ritz)” and “Batallón de modistillas (Seamstress Battalion)” on the base.

“My goal was to have this kind of music that she could be influenced by when talking to the soldiers and to create this evocative thing, like making two men play together as a couple,” Refree explains of the first number. “The second one needed a dirty energy that would make the soldiers lose control and want to touch her. So we created something very rhythmic and feminine at the same time.”

Wardrobe also helped shape Nené. Cobos drew from research on 19th century cuplé singers, like María del Consuelo Vello Cano, to sketch Cruz’s feathery, embellished ensemble.

“When she saw the white dress, she said, ‘This is it; she has to be wearing white,’ and Gris also said it would be helpful because we don’t have a lot of lights in the theater,” says Cobos. “[For the second number,] because I know Penélope’s a big fan of Mugler, I went through their images and found these overalls and dresses that reminded me of military costumes. So I did my own interpretation of them, and it worked really well because it helped her move and it was very comfortable for her to do the choreography with the tank and all of the other dancers.”

In the final scene of the film, Sebastián reads a poem with the words, “Mira, que nos acechan todavia (Beware, they still haunt us),” after being forced to shoot Rafael. It’s the final verse of “Love Sleeps in the Poet’s Breast,” one of the Sonetos del Amor Oscuro (Sonnets of Dark Love), written by Lorca for Rafael, expressing his forbidden desire for him amid the looming threat of societal persecution.

For Calvo, the warning is just as relevant today. “We had it on the whiteboard, and it was kind of a guide [for us],” he says. “Putting it [at the end of the film] was perfect and so significant because they do still haunt us.”

Apple HomePod mini 2 review: A necessary, but underwhelming update

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I'd be willing to forgive you if you forgot about the new HomePod mini. The overhauled smart speaker played a supporting role at best during Apple's Welcome Home event last month, behind the HomeView, and probably, in some minds, behind the updated Apple TV 4K. Still, the company provides a few upgrades in the HomePod mini 2 ($149), ensuring that it's ready for Siri AI and the assistant's newfound ability to offer personalized context for your queries. The main problem? The speaker doesn't yet support the recently upgraded virtual assistant Apple spent years improving.

The most notable update on the HomePod mini 2 is on the inside. Apple swapped in its S10 chip from the Apple Watch Series 10, kicking the nearly antique S5 silicon to the curb. For a Watch comparison, that's like going from a Series 5 or first-gen Watch SE, which debuted in 2019 and 2020 respectively, to the 2024 edition. It's a big leap, and it's one Apple says was necessary for the HomePod mini to work with Siri AI.

The recently updated assistant isn't all the S10 is helping with here. Apple says the component can run real-time music evaluation "to optimize dynamic range, loudness and acoustic performance," with a hand from "advanced computational analysis." When connected to the newly introduced fourth-gen Apple TV 4K, you can use four HomePod mini 2 speakers as a surround sound setup. That S10 chip helps the speakers recognize their positions in a room and adjust the lag, convey that info to Apple's streaming box, and audio levels accordingly.

I only have two HomePod mini 2s, so I wasn't able to test this configuration, but I did hear it during Apple's event in October. Even with that brief experience, I can tell you using two second-gen HomePods as the front speakers and two HomePod minis as the rears is a much better setup. The HomePod mini doesn't muster enough bass to be a great home theater speaker on its own, and those full-size HomePods bring the boom when an action scene demands it.

Before now, you could send audio from a Mac to a HomePod mini, but Apple has expanded the functionality so that the second-gen model can serve as persistent default speakers. This means, in theory, they'll stay connected to your Mac as the primary output rather than just relying on a temporary AirPlay link. This allows you to use the HomePod mini 2, or a pair of them, for everything on your computer — from FaceTime calls to games — without having to think about connecting each app or session to the speaker(s).

The HomePod line has always had lights on the top, but they haven't really done much. Before now, the top touch panel would light up if audio was playing or display an animation when you chatted with Siri. With a new feature called Sound Glow, those lights now show hues that compliment a song's album artwork. The lights move in real time, reacting to the bass and treble in each tune. If you opt for a soundscape, Sound Glow reacts to that too, showing an animation that resembles the pitter patter of rain drops, for example. It isn't a game-changing feature, but it's a nice use of lighting that was barely utilized previously.

Lastly, there are the new colors. The HomePod mini 2 comes in pink, green, blue, white and midnight (dark grey), with those first three coordinating well with Apple's iMac colors. That list means the company retired orange and yellow from the previous generation, both of which looked great in their time. As you can see, my review units are green, and I've enjoyed the pop of color they've added to my desk over the last few weeks.

Given that Apple saw fit to bestow a "2" on this HomePod mini, I expected some upgrades to overall sound quality to be in the mix. Unfortunately, I can't notice a difference between old and new models. Sometimes I think there's some variation, but it's not substantial enough that I'm sure, and it's not consistent across various musical styles. I do like the audio performance of the HomePod mini for its size, but it still has a sound profile of crisp highs and prominent mids offset by bass tone that lacks dynamics and depth. It's good enough for casual listening or background tunes, but not for anything more than that.

Despite the lack of Siri AI, I was hoping the S10 chip would provide a performance boost for the old Siri experience you have to settle for right now. However, the assistant's responses to basic questions took the same amount of time as they do on the first-gen model. To be clear, they're quick on both devices, I'm merely pointing out there's no difference in this regard. Just don't try to ask the old assistant to dive into Apple's core apps for you, or try to have an on-going conversation — neither request will bear any fruit.

The biggest issue with the HomePod mini 2 right now is its biggest omission: Siri AI. The recently updated assistant isn't available on the speaker yet, and Apple hasn't provided a timeline for its arrival either. The upgraded HomePod mini is equipped to support the now-current-gen Siri, thanks to the S10 chip. However, you'll have to wait for Siri's newfound contextual awareness to dive into your Messages, Calendar and other apps on your iPhone or iPad. And to me, that's a huge miss for Apple on an overhauled smart speaker. You'll also have to wait for the more conversational chats with Siri as the HomePod mini 2 doesn't yet support those either.

I like the idea of using a HomePod mini 2 or a stereo pair of them as a persistent computer speaker with a Mac, but it's not a great experience in practice. Any time you do anything — start a song, pause a song, skip tracks — there's a 1-2 second delay in the audio responding. The lights on the top of the speakers, including the Sound Glow effect, will stop shining immediately, but the audio has to catch up. There's also a slight delay between speakers if you're using a stereo pair, which sounds like every song has added reverb. For these reasons, I found the Mac speaker feature more frustrating than enjoyable, and I quickly moved back to AirPlay over a nearby Sonos speaker for my productivity tunes. And I haven't even mentioned the fact that I can't get the persistent connection to stick with my MacBook Air either. I'm constantly reselecting the HomePod mini 2 as the audio output.

Another item that's disappointing is something else that's not here: a substantial overhaul. The original HomePod mini is six years old at this point, so I expected a much bigger redesign for the second-gen version (until the rumor mill squashed that). If you have a first-gen HomePod mini, you'll miss out on the updated Siri, whenever it arrives, and new features like Sound Glow and the home theater option. And if you're hoping to create a stereo pair, you'll need two of the same generation HomePod minis to do that; you can't pair a first-gen speaker with a second-gen model for that purpose.

Lastly, there's the price. The previous version was $99 before Apple raised it to $129 in June when the company hiked the prices on pretty much all of its hardware. Now, I'm well aware of the global economic factors at play, especially the AI-fueled cost increase on RAM and other components, but asking $50 more than the first-gen model for incremental updates is tough. Had Apple kept the price at the June rate, the HomePod mini 2 would be a much easier recommendation.

It's baffling to me that Apple would announce an updated HomePod mini without support for the latest version of Siri ready to go. The speaker's primary use is a hands-free conduit to the company's voice assistant, after all. The only decent conclusion I can come to is that Apple wanted to announce what it considered was a full suite of home products around the HomeView debut, and it felt like the Apple TV 4K, HomePod mini and a collection of smart home devices were the best way to do that. I would be interested to know what the hold up is, because the Apple Watch Series 10 supports Siri AI already, and those wearables are also running the same S10 chip as the HomePod mini 2. But, of course, Apple isn't talking specifics.

After spending a few weeks with the HomePod mini 2, I mostly wish Apple had just waited. At a higher price, this isn't as compelling as it used to be in terms of getting Siri in your home. And with Siri AI missing, and some of the new audio features proving to be unreliable, it's clear a bit more polish was needed here.

Is This the Beginning of the End for Modi?

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Is This the Beginning of the End for Modi?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Thousands took to the streets in New Delhi last month to protest irregularities in electoral rolls and alleged election fraud. Reports claimed that more than 130 million voters were deleted from electoral lists, in ways that would benefit Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP). But will this anger in New Delhi spread to the rest of the country, weakening Modi’s 12-year hold on power, or will it simply fizzle out as time passes and other concerns become urgent?

A lot depends on India’s opposition parties and whether they can keep up the momentum until elections in 2029 and offer a viable alternative to the people—but equally on how the populace rates Modi’s performance. Has he raised their standard of living? Is the opposition right in alleging that the BJP is systematically hollowing out Indian democracy? Increasingly, those questions are being answered in a way that makes it seem like Modi has peaked.

Thousands took to the streets in New Delhi last month to protest irregularities in electoral rolls and alleged election fraud. Reports claimed that more than 130 million voters were deleted from electoral lists, in ways that would benefit Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP). But will this anger in New Delhi spread to the rest of the country, weakening Modi’s 12-year hold on power, or will it simply fizzle out as time passes and other concerns become urgent?

A lot depends on India’s opposition parties and whether they can keep up the momentum until elections in 2029 and offer a viable alternative to the people—but equally on how the populace rates Modi’s performance. Has he raised their standard of living? Is the opposition right in alleging that the BJP is systematically hollowing out Indian democracy? Increasingly, those questions are being answered in a way that makes it seem like Modi has peaked.

At a Diwali party last month, a young Indian researcher in her mid-20s warned me against attending a protest rally. “The police will pick you up,” she said, her eyes wide open. In a recent protest, the Delhi police had locked down major parts of the capital and cracked down on protesters. The police cut off the internet, shut down local trains in key areas, and detained thousands, including journalists. Even those hanging around weren’t spared. “Our lawyer friends who went to the protest site to help the protesters were also beaten up,” she said. “Because our friends were detained and beaten, now we fear we could be next. You could be next.”

This is starkly different from India in 2011, when tens of thousands flocked to Jantar Mantar, a historical protest site near Delhi’s power center, for an anti-corruption demonstration. That protest paved the way for the Congress-led government’s defeat and Modi’s entry into national politics in 2014. Modi’s government, however, appears to have drawn a very different lesson about how to deal with protests.

BJP leaders suggested removing protesters because they inconvenienced passersby and businesses. That line was parroted relentlessly in TV studios. In my travels in and around Delhi, I found that message landed with certain people—mostly those who also believed the BJP’s insinuation, which came without any evidence, that protests in India were a foreign ploy to destabilize the country. Protests in India unfolded at the same time as in France and elsewhere in Europe, and this was portrayed as a sign of collaboration.

However, there appears to be a pattern in how the current Indian government discredits and responds to protests. A few months ago, when Indian students marched against an exam paper leak and jobless growth, the same police thrashed the students, even firing pellet guns. Some TV anchors claimed the police were the victims.

These are unusual scenes in India, a democratic country where the right to protest is guaranteed under the constitution. India won its independence through peaceful protests against the British.

On Nov. 1, despite the police’s reported highhandedness, thousands of people from different parts of the country gathered for the mega protest at an alternative and government-approved protest site. By noon, as I reached Connaught Place in central Delhi, Congress workers were marching on the streets and calling for the removal of Chief Election Commissioner Gyanesh Kumar. They accused Kumar of working to benefit the BJP.

But Kumar’s role and the discrepancies in electoral rolls weren’t the only reasons that pushed people to protest. Three Gen Z protesters stood in a corner, two of them wearing masks to avoid being identified by the police. “They picked up people after the protests last time, from their homes,” said one of the masked boys. All three had joined the protests for very different reasons but the same cause—saving Indian democracy.

The masked boy recounted the story of how his friend, a child, was terrified for his mother’s security when Hindu-Muslim riots unfolded in Delhi early 2020. He blamed the BJP for instigating the riots. The young man standing to his left hailed from West Bengal and was furious that his grandparents, both octogenarians, were knocked off the voters list and denied the right to cast their ballot. “[The government] wanted to exclude a certain community, Bengali Muslims that they falsely say are Bangladeshis, but everyone was affected,” he said. “They are dividing us on the basis of religion; this is not okay.”

Mihika Omseema refused to cover up her face. She said her parents supported her and that gave her the strength to reveal her identity and take part in protests. She said she joined to protest against growing Hindu-Muslim polarization, falling economic standards, exam paper leaks, and a whole host of other issues. In Delhi, some neighborhoods showed a mood where parents might join their children—if not at the protest grounds, then at the ballot. A senior gentleman and an upper-caste Hindu said, “Modi will not win Delhi, no chance,” but he confessed that Modi still had many supporters in the country. An 80-kilometer ride to Meerut has been shortened from more than 3 hours to less than 2 hours via a new flyover completed under Modi’s government, which has also built several other key highways linking cities in western Uttar Pradesh. Meerut, for the first time, has a fast train that reaches Delhi in less than an hour and thus expands employment potential. (Uttar Pradesh has the maximum number of electoral constituencies and plays a decisive role in forming the government in Delhi. Although central elections are still more than two years away, the state itself will go to the polls next year.)

In Meerut, I heard a different set of complaints against the BJP. Younger people acknowledged infrastructure development but said there was still no work. Only Muslims were worried about growing polarization; Hindus were more upset with slow economic growth and a demolition drive to bulldoze illegal shops and other structures. “Of course, this will affect the BJP in the elections; people are, we are, very angry with the government,” said Aditya Kumar, who sat on a stool in his emptied and recently destroyed shop in a market where rubble had still not been cleared. He sold nuts, bolts, and other construction material. “The structures were illegal, but there is no other employment, so what were we supposed to do?” he said, highlighting the lack of industry and compulsion to resort to small retail businesses.

“This is dictatorship,” said Kumar, a staunch Modi supporter until the demolition drive. “And no one raises our voice because the media doesn’t broadcast anything critical of Modi.”

About a dozen other upper-caste Hindus in the city who professed to be pro-Modi said the BJP was unlikely to win Meerut even if it retains the state. That could change if there were credible opposition, but they did not see a match. Congress scion Rahul Gandhi marched alongside protesters and promised to seek justice for those wrongfully detained or beaten up. In Delhi, Gen Z protesters spoke in his favor. But in a state deeply divided along caste lines, despite being upper caste, he is still not seen as an alternative.

Akash, a 26-year-old from Bulandshahr, said that the BJP will return to power because it had ensured quick payment for sugarcane to farmers like his father. “There are also more roads and police stations,” he said, emphasizing improvement in law and order under the BJP government led by Modi acolyte Yogi Adityanath. He said that Indians like him would rate Modi on economic development over the protests in Delhi or the state of Indian democracy. But he also didn’t want to give up his own right to protest or question the government.

“If the choice is between a better economy and democracy, I choose the economy, but if it is between the economy and my ability to question the government, then I choose democracy,” he said.

Like Meerut in Uttar Pradesh, in the Shekhawati region of Rajasthan, local factors were adding to a sense that all was not well under the Modi government. But here, too, the protests were not the reason that people said the government could change. “Farmers are not being paid on time,” Bhawani Singh, a tourist taxi driver, said. “Our children will never go and protest like those in Delhi. But we are upset about the Agnipath scheme and the shortening of military service,” he said, referring to government plans to cut down the length of service below the rank of officer to four years and retain only a quarter for a full term. “Most kids here want to join the army or the police,” he said. (The government appears to have noticed the grievance, announcing earlier this month that two more years of service could be offered and a second chance at joining longer-term,, for those who meet the selection criteria.)

Local factors remain paramount for Indians deciding how Modi has fared in his third term. The protests have had some effect on their overall assessment—distancing those affected by other government policies, while many remain ambivalent or supportive. The weakening of institutions isn’t yet a factor in how they vote, but it has been registered, as former supporters say the media is largely pliable, and now the Election Commission is suspected of being subservient.

The fence-sitters are the upper-caste Hindus who form the backbone of the BJP’s voter base. And while many are still firmly behind the party and backing Hindu dominance, at least some appeared uncomfortable with a slide away from democracy. “He wants to be Putin,” an upper-caste Hindu said  . “Now with the delimitation bill, the BJP is trying to ensure it always has enough seats to keep them in power,” he added, referring to legislation that will come up for voting in parliament next month and intends to expand the number of seats based on population. The same exercise in Jammu and Kashmir, Chief Minister Omar Abdullah said, helped the BJP.

The recent protests are occurring in the middle of Modi’s current stint in office. He still has time to assuage concerns. But the opposition will also have a chance to highlight its case about the weakening of democratic institutions and the threat to Indian democracy. And just as a political wave pushed Modi into office 12 years ago, another one could be building that will eventually sweep him out.

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

What Are Perpetual Futures? How Crypto's Most Popular Derivative Works

Perpetual futures are the engine room of crypto trading. Centralized exchanges processed $85.3 trillion in perps trading volume in 2025 alone, according to CoinGecko's State of Crypto Perpetuals Report 2026, and the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) describes perps as the dominant form of crypto derivatives trading.

They aren't only for crypto, either. Exchanges now offer perps on stocks, commodities and currencies, and Decrypt has become the first news publisher to offer perps trading, powered by Solana exchange Phoenix.

This guide explains what futures are, what makes perpetual futures different, how funding rates and liquidations work, and what to know before trading them.

To understand perpetual futures, it helps to start with ordinary futures.

A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell an asset at a set price on a set date in the future. Futures have been used for centuries: farmers used them to lock in prices for crops before harvest, and today they are traded on everything from oil and wheat to stock indexes and Bitcoin, on regulated exchanges such as CME.

Traders use futures for two main reasons. Hedgers use them to protect against price swings; an airline might buy oil futures to lock in fuel costs, for example. Speculators use them to bet on price movements without owning the underlying asset.

Traditional futures have an expiry date. When a contract expires, it settles, either by delivering the asset or, more often, by paying out the difference in cash. Traders who want to keep their position open must "roll" it into a new contract, which can add costs and complexity.

Perpetual futures, often shortened to "perps" or "perpetual swaps," work like futures, but with one key difference: they never expire. Traders can hold a position for as long as they like, provided they have enough collateral to keep it open.

The idea was proposed by Yale economist Robert Shiller in a 1993 paper as a way to create markets for assets that are hard to trade directly. Perps took off in crypto. Crypto exchange BitMEX, widely credited with bringing perps to crypto, launched a perpetual contract on Bitcoin in 2016. BitMEX itself closed in September 2026, but the product it pioneered has become the most traded instrument in crypto.

A perpetual contract never actually involves owning the asset. Traders deposit collateral, or "margin," and their profit or loss depends entirely on how the price moves.

When opening a perp position, a trader chooses a direction. Going long means betting the price will rise; going short means betting it will fall. Being able to short as easily as going long is one reason perps are popular: it lets traders profit from falling prices, or hedge assets they already own.

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets traders control a larger position than their collateral alone would allow. With 10x leverage, for example, $100 of margin controls a $1,000 position. If the asset's price rises 5%, the trader makes $50, a 50% return on their margin. But if it falls 5%, they lose $50, half of their margin.

Exchanges track two prices to manage this. The index price is the asset's price on spot markets, usually averaged across several exchanges. The mark price, based on the index and the perp market itself, is used to calculate profits and losses and to decide when positions must be closed. Using an averaged price makes it harder for a sudden spike on one exchange to trigger liquidations.

Because perps never expire, nothing forces their price to converge with the underlying asset, as traditional futures do at expiry. Instead, perps use a funding rate.

At regular intervals—most commonly every eight hours, though some platforms use shorter or longer schedules—traders on one side of the market pay traders on the other:

Decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, for example, pays funding every hour, at one-eighth of the rate calculated for an eight-hour period, to stay consistent with centralized exchanges.

Funding payments go directly between traders, not to the exchange. For traders holding positions over long periods, these payments can add up, and they can either boost or erode returns.

Funding rates also offer a window into market sentiment. Strongly positive funding suggests traders are crowded into bullish bets, while negative funding shows that bears are paying to stay short.

Rates for the same asset can also differ between exchanges. On November 19, 2026, Bitcoin's funding rate was 0.01% per eight hours on Hyperliquid, compared with 0.0037% on Binance, according to Hyperliquid's funding comparison tool.

Some traders aim to earn funding payments rather than bet on prices. In a "cash and carry" or basis trade, they buy an asset on the spot market and short the same amount through perps, collecting funding while their positions offset each other's price moves.

Leverage is a double-edged sword. Every perp position has a maintenance margin, the minimum collateral needed to keep it open. If losses push a trader's margin below that level, the exchange automatically closes the position, a process called liquidation. The trader typically loses most or all of the margin they put up.

The higher the leverage, the smaller the price move needed to trigger a liquidation. At 50x leverage, a 2% move against a position can wipe it out.

Liquidations can also feed on each other. When prices fall sharply, liquidated longs are forced to sell, pushing prices lower and triggering further liquidations, a so-called liquidation cascade. On October 10, 2025, the crypto market saw its largest liquidation event on record, with more than $19 billion in leveraged positions wiped out in a single day.

To cover losses when positions can't be closed in time, exchanges typically keep an insurance fund. In extreme cases, some use auto-deleveraging, closing profitable positions on the other side of the market to keep the system solvent.

Crypto perps trade on two main types of venue.

Centralized exchanges, such as Binance, Bybit and OKX, run their own order books and hold users' funds. They account for the vast majority of perps trading: Binance alone held about a third of centralized perps volume in early 2026, according to CoinGecko. Many are unavailable to U.S. customers.

Decentralized exchanges let traders use perps through smart contracts, while keeping control of their funds. Some, such as Hyperliquid and Solana-based Phoenix, run order books onchain, matching buyers and sellers much like a traditional exchange. Others, such as GMX and Jupiter, let traders trade against a shared pool of liquidity provided by other users.

Decentralized perps are growing fast. Their trading volume jumped from $1.5 trillion in 2024 to $6.38 trillion in 2025, led by Hyperliquid, CoinGecko found. Decentralized exchanges' share of perps trading volume peaked at 13% in late 2025 before slipping to 10% in April 2026, while their share of open interest, the total value of open positions, reached 13.5%.

In the U.S., perps developed largely on offshore exchanges, outside the reach of the CFTC, but that has started to change. In July 2025, Coinbase introduced CFTC-regulated "perpetual-style" futures for U.S. customers, with five-year expiries and up to 10x leverage. Two months later, CFTC-regulated exchange Bitnomial launched what it called the first U.S. perpetual futures, with 25-year terms and eight-hour funding. In May 2026, the CFTC approved a Bitcoin perpetual futures contract from prediction market Kalshi and set out a framework for listing more, and in June, Kraken rolled out perps to eligible U.S. customers through Bitnomial, which its parent company had acquired.

Some of these contracts, including Kalshi's and Bitnomial's original design, have long but fixed terms of 25 years, so are not strictly perpetual. The regulator says perps can be offered on CFTC-registered exchanges under federal oversight, and has advised U.S. traders to avoid offshore venues.

The shift has been contested. CME Group, the largest U.S. futures exchange, has sued the CFTC, arguing that perps are swaps rather than futures under U.S. law. The CFTC has nonetheless continued to approve more perps platforms, starting with Backpack.

Perps are now spreading beyond crypto. Exchanges have begun listing perps tracking stocks, stock indexes, commodities such as gold and oil, and currencies, giving traders around-the-clock exposure to markets that normally close in the evening and on weekends.

Those products carry the same mechanics, and the same risks, as crypto perps. They track an asset's price without giving traders ownership of the asset itself, so a perp on a company's stock doesn't come with voting rights or dividends.

Perps can be useful tools for hedging and speculation, but they are among the riskiest products in crypto:

Decrypt users can now trade perps directly alongside the news, through the Decrypt Money Account. The service is powered by Phoenix, a decentralized exchange on Solana, and lets users go long or short on crypto, stocks and commodities.

Availability varies by region, and as with any leveraged product, traders should only risk money they can afford to lose.

Decrypt offers perpetual futures trading through the Decrypt Money Account, powered by Phoenix.

Perpetual futures are leveraged products and carry a high risk of loss. Availability may vary by region. Nothing in this guide is investment advice.

My Hostile Hostel Experience

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Now playing: “Stubborn Love” by The Lumineers

I stayed next to a shelter where the first murder on the Appalachian took place. Later that day, I received my first instance on the LASH of roadside trail magic. The next day, I set my personal record for miles completed within a certain timeframe without the original intent to do so. Along the way, I met some trail maintenance volunteers. I ended the day at Blue Mountain Shelter with April, Nathan and Pete, among others.

I started my morning very positively. As usual, I was up in time to see another beautiful sunrise. Today I was set to arrive at Green Dragon Hostel. First, I hiked a short 2.5 miles – some downhill – to Unicoi Gap, where April, Nathan, Pete and I got picked up by a shuttle. The three of them were on trail much earlier than me, as I was still working out a better system to pack up camp with legitimate ADHD (Predominantly Inattentive). With Nate and April already at the parking lot, I caught up with Pete just as we reached the road crossing. The shuttle driver was already there waiting, and that’s when my good mood changed.

Let me start by saying I’m about to sound negative. (What a great thing to hear, right?) But as I’ve mentioned in previous posts, these are my thoughts I had in the moments I’m sharing. I don’t want to sugarcoat my memories to be glamorous if that’s not how they were. First and foremost, I write these blog posts for me; to remember everything I went through (good or bad), smile at the good times, learn from the bad times, and use it all for motivation to keep going no matter what. Yada yada blah blah blah.

After a positive start to the day, I was rushed into the SUV before even getting a chance to catch my breath – quite literally. And then I was told I was asking too many questions. Nate and April essentially gave the driver permission to make jokes at my expense, but I wasn’t in that kind of mood. I simply wanted to plan out my nero so that I could be ready in time for the morning shuttle back to the trailhead. Instead, I wasn’t taken seriously and was told to relax. But this ain’t my first rodeo. Having ADHD-predominantly inattentive means my preparation takes way longer than others. My attitude became, “Don’t say I didn’t warn you.” I was an overwhelmed grump already.

But the day got better fast. I got a text from my fellow blogger for TheTrek, 007, saying he was close by. So after getting the tour of the beautiful hostel, those of us already here showered and headed into the town of Hiawassee for lunch and resupply at the grocery stores. It was a nice town. But the highlight for me was the restaurant Bronco’s, specializing in burgers, barbecue and burritos. I got the dry rub wings, fries and a regular cheeseburger with pickles and ketchup. Phenomenal! Take me back.

Well, I did go back for dinner. But not before regrouping back at the hostel, where two other hikers showed up. One of them was named . The other was – wait, it’s him! My fellow blogger buddy, 007 ! He had been chasing me down for days now. We immediately hugged like long lost friends and shared around the campfire how our treks had been going.

It was great to finally meet him in person, but our relaxing time was short-lived. Unfortunately, the hostel owners were only planning one drop-off the next morning. And because I know it takes me a while to pack in the morning, I had to start the process tonight. Bags had to stay outside (usually to keep norovirus away), and quiet hours made it difficult to move around without disturbing others. Their process made sense, but it simply didn’t fit with my routine. Having ADHD-predominantly inattentive makes it tough for me to stay focused and on task. So without my checklist, I would literally stare at my pile of gear, overwhelmed at where to start.

Woe is me, I know. If I’m not “complaining” about being deaf in one ear, it’s my auditory processing disorder (APD) or ADHD-PI or one of my other defects that gets attention. But I can still backpack. And I won’t take that blessing for granted.

Morning came, and I was not ready on time. I was up and at ’em before the wake-up call, but it didn’t matter. I tried to scarf down some of the complimentary breakfast, and that was a mistake. Because the only shuttle to the trailhead was ready to leave, I had to stuff the rest of my stuff not yet packed into a plastic bag and finish packing at the trailhead. Not gonna lie: It was more stressful than it should have been for the cost of hostel stays. I’m still grateful for all that was provided, though.

“Keep your head up, my love,” the Lumineers sing in their song about perseverance and self-encouragement. The lyrics were certainly on my mind that morning.

I hate writing negatively. I’m sure it’s not fun for you readers, either. And I don’t want my not-so-great experience at such a renowned hostel to turn other hikers away from what will probably be one of the best experiences for those not in my scatterbrained position.

But I also believe it’s important to keep my experiences genuine and show that things get better. And on days when I’m not having fun, I remember one of the golden rules of backpacking: Never quit on a bad day. And I’m glad I didn’t, because two other friends were about to make my day at the trailhead.

I keep going, of course. My spirits are immediately lifted when I meet yet another blogger and reunite with a friend I met on Day 1.

Ben Zartman Splices Climbing Slings In His Garage. They’re the Best I’ve Ever Used.

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ben Zartman at work splicing slings in his garage. (Photo: Courtesy Ben Zartman)

When Ben Zartman learned to climb near Mexico City in the early 1990s, no one he knew could afford climbing gear.

“All of us were poor, and all the gear was imported and so expensive,” he says. “Most of the guys at the crag were sewing their own shoes or making their own cams.” Backpacks, harnesses, quickdraws—if they could sew it themselves, they did. At the time, Zartman was in high school. He didn’t have a job, but he did have a knack for making stuff. It never occurred to him that doing so might be sketchy until he arrived in Yosemite for the first time in 1995 and partners refused to climb on his rack. They were astonished he’d made his own gear. He was astonished that they’d bought theirs.

Later that year, when a government shutdown forced Zartman out of Joshua Tree, he hitchhiked to San Diego and bummed a ride to Baja with some sailors. Over the next few years, he split his time between big walls and boats, getting better at both kinds of rigging all the while. Eventually he got married, had three daughters, and spent five years sailing around the world on a boat he’d built himself. Eventually, he got work in a rigging shop in Rhode Island. He spliced custom slings for workaday sailors, then for open-ocean racing boats.

“At one point, I was sitting in the shop, splicing these high-end Dyneema loops, and I thought, man, if I had these in Yosemite, I would have been the coolest kid at the lodge cafeteria,” he says. And he likely would have: the spliced, covered loops used in sailing are seamless: no bar tacks or stitching. Instead, one end of the loop is woven into the other like a snake eating its tail. When you pull, the ends suction against each other (Zartman compares it to a finger-trap toy), making the loop stronger under load than bartacked Dyneema.

“The idea fomented for a while in my mind, and in 2021 I started reaching out to every climbing gear manufacturer I could think of,” Zartman says. Letters and samples went out to Black Diamond, Metolius, Sterling, and others, asking them if they’d be interested in partnering on a product. Hardly anyone responded.

So Zartman did what he’d always done: he took the DIY approach. In 2023, he launched his company, Zartman Rigging. He joined the UIAA, mailed in slings for lab testing, and registered for UIAA labels. Once his gear was certified, the renowned gear shop IME, in North Conway, New Hampshire, started selling it. Rock and Snow in New Paltz, New York, and IME in Salt Lake City, Utah, were next. As his following grew, so did his offerings: a featherlight personal anchor system, seamless rappel back-ups, a rope-carabiner alternative called the Dreamlink, and a tiny rappel plate designed for 6-mm cord. The ideas come from Zartman’s experience climbing Yosemite walls; the materials come from his time on boats.

“The research and development budget in the [sailboat] racing scene is so big,” he says. “It’s a great place to take technology from. I’m at that edge—I make ropes for the fanciest racing boats in the world, so I’m able to bring that into the climbing world pretty quickly.”

The results are wild. The Dreamlink has a breaking strength of 43 kN (or, rather, he tested it up to 42 kN in a UIAA lab, and that’s when it broke their machine). The seamless runners are tagged 24 kN but break at 27kN. A fancy UV coating makes them essentially sun-proof. They don’t expire like other soft goods; you only have to retire them if the core becomes exposed. I have a double-length seamless runner that I use for building anchors and extending rappels. No bartack means easy setup, it racks well, and it’s pretty light. (Most traditionally manufactured Dyneema slings are tagged at 22kN. Mammut’s 120-centimeter Contact Sling weighs 28 grams, while Zartman’s version is 32 grams, the same as Black Diamond’s Infinity Cord.)

Zartman has a strong social media fan club, but he has plenty of critics too. Mountain Project commenters, he said, can be particularly vicious. Many scoff at the idea of a soft shackle or a seamless runner as gimmicky or unsafe. He’s not too bothered.

“Ray Jardine got scolded by all the Valley locals when he showed up with Friends,” he says, citing the first-ever cams. When other companies started making camming devices in the ‘80s, Jardine’s critics were forced to bite their tongues. Zartman is seeing a similar arc with his own gear. Several other climbing brands have started marketing soft shackles similar to Zartman’s Dreamlink. And in 2025, Black Diamond released its Infinity Cord, a Dyneema sling that uses the same bartack-free construction as Zartman’s and the same covered-loop splicing technique that sailors have used for decades. Black Diamond called it a “breakthrough in the evolution of cordelette engineering.”

Zartman says he’s unaffected by the new competition. “If there are people copying the concept, that’s good because that means the concept is good,” he says. In the meantime, he’s keeping his head down, focused on fulfilling orders and prototyping his next batch of inventions.

Zartman Rigging is gaining recognition, albeit slowly. Zartman does no paid advertising; about 80 percent of his business comes from Instagram. His top-shelf pricing likely tempers growth as well. For context, a 120-centimeter Zartman Rigging sling is $63, versus $40 for Black Diamond’s Infinity Cord or $14 for a basic Dyneema Trango runner of the same length.

“Because I make everything out of the nicest materials and by hand, it’s the most expensive gear on the market. It’s a luxury brand,” he says. “Which is ironic given that I started in Mexico with my own homemade climbing shoes.”

Still, he produces enough to keep busy. Recently, he was able to hire one of his daughters part-time to help him splice, tag, and quality-test each product. He’s now a member of the UIAA safety commission, helping the organization design new testing protocols around skinny ropes and other novel forms of cord. He’s shipping to Alaska and Hawaii. People have started recognizing him in gear shops and at crags. Things are taking off—despite what Mountain Projectors may say.

“To a lot of people’s surprise,” he laughs, “I’ve sold a lot of gear.”

All the Country Stars in Taylor Swift’s CMA Awards Performance, Explained

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

We already knew that when Taylor Swift returned to the CMAs for the first time in 13 years, the night was going to be packed with easter eggs. What we didn’t know was that her performance would be far more than just that, filled with appearances from country stars who have all played an important role in her storied career since it kicked off in Nashville with her self-titled debut in 2006.

Swift began the performance of her nominated song “I Knew It, I Knew You” in her dressing room (with outfits from her Taylor Swift era scattered across the room, mind you) before making her way backstage at the Bridgestone Arena en route to the stage. Here, we walk through all the artists she encountered along the way.

As Swift rounded the corner from her dressing room, there he was: Brad Paisley, polishing a handful of CMA Awards. Paisley had already made a surprise appearance earlier in the night to join Lainey Wilson for a musical monologue, but his significance in Swift’s country music story is substantial. Swift opened for Paisley on his Bonfires & Amplifiers Tour in 2007, where he apparently played a prank on the young artist, releasing crickets on her tour bus. “For months, I couldn’t sleep,” Swift said in October when she was inducted into the Nashville Songwriters Hall of Fame. Clearly there’s no, er, bad blood remaining.

Up next in Swift’s stroll were Little Big Town, the foursome of Karen Fairchild, Kimberly Schlapman, Phillip Sweet, and Jimi Westbrook. Little Big Town had a hit in 2016 with a version of Swift’s song “Better Man,” which was originally crafted for Red (Swift eventually recorded her own take of the solo write as a vault track). “I’m grateful for her friendship through the years,” Fairchild told Rolling Stone, “and we love her so much. She found her better man.”

Swift did a little dance with Kristian Bush and Jennifer Nettles of Sugarland, a duo with whom she has a longstanding relationship. Swift opened for Sugarland in 2007, and even owns a signed copy of their debut EP, Premium Quality Tunes. A decade later, when Swift was looking for someone to sing a song she’d written, “Babe,” she called up the twosome. They ended up recording it for their album Bigger. Swift also served as a guest on the track, even appearing in the dramatic music video. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Swift grabbed a burgundy cowboy hat from Brooks & Dunn as she passed them in the hall, a duo she would later honor with a speech when she presented them with the Willie Nelson Lifetime Achievement Award. “That’s the thing about Brooks & Dunn,” Swift said. “They don’t take very much too seriously, except for all the important stuff: their families, their fans, and country music.” Swift is clearly a fan of Kix Brooks and Ronnie Dunn, covering songs like their Number One hit “Ain’t Nothing ‘Bout You” live.

That burgundy hat? Swift didn’t keep it for long, dropping it on the head of CMA Awards host Wilson. The two artists posed on the red carpet together before the show in complementary yellow dresses (an easter egg? Maybe!), and have performed together before, sharing the stage for a version of Swift’s “Love Story” at her husband Travis Kelce’s Tight End University event earlier this year. Wilson also sung the same Swift classic at her induction into the Nashville Songwriter’s Hall of Fame.

The next cameo is one drenched in a bit of Swiftian lore: Eric Church. Swift popped on a pair of aviators as she passed the Chief, bumping fists as he leaned against a column. One of Swift’s biggest opportunities early in her career was opening for Rascal Flatts, a gig she snagged only after Church, who refused to conform his set to the band’s time requirements, was fired. It worked out for the best for both of them, obviously. Trending Stories John Mayer Transports Michael J. Fox Back to the Future With Chuck Berry Performance CMA Awards 2026: The Complete Winners List Halestorm, Brothers Osborne, Hardy Sing for Brooks & Dunn at Ferocious 2026 CMA Tribute Stephen Wilson Jr. and Chris Stapleton Collab on ‘I’m a Song’ at 2026 CMA Awards Related Content Taylor Swift’s CMA Performance Was Filled With Easter Eggs From the Early Days of Her Career CMA Awards 2026: The Complete Winners List Stephen Wilson Jr. and Chris Stapleton Collab on ‘I’m a Song’ at 2026 CMA Awards See Miranda Lambert, Ella Langley Give Live Debut of ‘Snakeskin Boots’ at 2026 CMAs

Though he may not be as recognizable as the others — at least outside of Nashville, where he’s revered as country music royalty — the moment of “Whispering Bill” Anderson handing Swift her guitar was truly touching, and loaded with lore. The family friend and Country Music Hall of Famer invited Swift to play at his fan club party in 2004 when she was just a teenager, sharing the stage at the Grand Ole Opry in 2010 where Anderson is currently the longest standing member. This move may be the most symbolic of all when it comes to the potential of Swift returning to her country music roots, and one that shows how deep her love and knowledge of the genre really runs. The duo blowing each other a kiss was, well, the chef’s kiss of it all.

When Swift finally took the stage for her grand return to the CMA Awards, she wasn’t going to do it alone. Keith Urban, on his trusty ganjo, joined Swift for the last few bars of “I Knew It, I Knew You” before launching into “Our Song,” which she also performed at the CMAs in 2007. Urban and Swift go back to 2009, when she opened his tour, and he also appears on Fearless (Taylor’s Version), lending vocals to “That’s When” and “We Were Happy.” Urban was more than content to just be a supportive player for his old friend, picking at his instrument and hopping dutifully through the dance moves. Are all of these reunions a tribute to her past, or a hint to her future? Knowing Swift, it’s surely some version of both.

Peter Moore says this is "more than the release of a hugely anticipated game"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As GTA 6 brings in rave reviews and – predictably – takes over the internet as millions of players dive into Rockstar's long-awaited blockbuster, industry veteran Peter Moore says "the video game industry could really use a moment like this."

Moore, who previously served as president of Sega of America, corporate vice president of Microsoft's home and entertainment division responsible for Xbox marketing and operations, and CEO of EA, has taken to Twitter in the wake of Rockstar's latest launch. First, he offers an AI-modified image of himself with a GTA 6 tattoo in place of his infamous GTA 4 tattoo, which was revealed at E3 2006 to confirm the game's simultaneous launch on Xbox 360 and PS3.

But "in all seriousness," he adds, GTA 6's launch is an important one, and not just for the fans who've been waiting 13 years to play a new Grand Theft Auto game.

"We've been through a challenging period," he acknowledges. "Studio closures, redundancies, canceled projects and enormous uncertainty have taken their toll on an industry that has given so much to so many, myself included.

"That's why the launch of GTA VI feels so significant," he continues. "It's more than the release of a hugely anticipated game. It's an opportunity to remind the world of the extraordinary power of this industry to create, innovate, entertain and bring people together."

As someone who's spent so much time in the games industry, Moore says, "I've seen this industry reinvent itself time and time again." With that in mind, he hopes GTA 6 is able to deliver "the commercial success we all expect, but perhaps more importantly, I hope it provides a much-needed shot in the arm (no pun intended…) for the entire ecosystem: developers, publishers, studios, retailers, technology partners and the next generation of creative talent." After all, he says, "The games industry has always been at its best when it dares to dream big."

Well, as far as things go on the commercial front, it looks like the game has had an extremely strong start. Rockstar hasn't revealed any official figures, but with analysts' old launch week predictions going as high as $5.2 billion, and more recent estimates placing the revenue as of yesterday at $1.65 billion, it's safe that plenty of folks are buying it.
